\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\underbrace{s_{\lambda/\mu}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)}_{=s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)s_{\lambda/\mu}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\underbrace{s_{\lambda/\mu}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=s_{\lambda/\mu}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)s_{\lambda/\mu},

we obtain

\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)s_{\lambda/\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)h_{\lambda}.

Consider this as an equality in the ring
\Lambda\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]=\Lambda\left[\left[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
. Apply the map
\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]:\Lambda\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]
to
both sides of this equality. We obtain

\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)s_{\lambda/\mu}\right)=\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)h_{\lambda}\right).

Comparing this with

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)s_{\lambda/\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(s_{\lambda/\mu}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\alpha_{k}\left(s_{\lambda/\mu}\right)\\
\text{(since }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{ extends }\alpha_{k}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the map }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{ preserves infinite }\mathbf{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{-linear combinations}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\left(s_{\lambda/\mu}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.alks}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\cdot\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\cdot s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right),

we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\cdot s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)h_{\lambda}\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\underbrace{\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\right)\left(h_{\lambda}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\alpha_{k}\left(h_{\lambda}\right)\\
\text{(since }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{ extends }\alpha_{k}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the map }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{ preserves infinite }\mathbf{k}\left[\left[\mathbf{y}\right]\right]\text{-linear combinations}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\left(h_{\lambda}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.main.alkh}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\cdot\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\right]\cdot s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{y}\right).

Renaming the indeterminates
\mathbf{y}=\left(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right)
as
\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right)
on
both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\cdot s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\right]\cdot\underbrace{s_{\mu}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=s_{\mu}}\underbrace{m_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=m_{\lambda}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\right]\cdot s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for
all }i\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\text{not
}\left(\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since we don't
have \textquotedblleft}\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\text{\textquotedblright)}\end{subarray}}\cdot s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since any }\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\text{ satisfies either }\left(\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right)\text{
or not }\left(\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for
all }i\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\text{not }\left(\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\right)\end{subarray}}0s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}m_{\lambda}.

Comparing this with

G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\mu}=s_{\mu}\cdot\underbrace{G\left(k\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by Proposition
\ref{prop.G.basics} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}=s_{\mu}\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\lambda_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\end{subarray}}s_{\mu}m_{\lambda},

we obtain

G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\cdot\underbrace{s_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=s_{\lambda}}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}.

This proves Theorem
2.16
.

3.9
Second proof of Theorem

2.16

Our second proof of Theorem
2.16
will rely on
[
GriRei20
, §2.6]
and specifically on the notion of alternants. We shall give only a
sketch of it; the reader can consult
[
Grinbe20b
]
for the full version.

Proof 3.18

(Second proof of Theorem
2.16
(sketched).)
.

If
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
is any formal
power series, and if
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, then
f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)
shall denote the formal power series

f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell},0,0,0,\ldots\right)\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]\right]

that is obtained by substituting
0,0,0,\ldots
for the variables
x_{\ell+1},x_{\ell+2},x_{\ell+3},\ldots
in
f
. Equivalently,
f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)
can be obtained from
f
by removing all
monomials that contain any of the variables
x_{\ell+1},x_{\ell+2},x_{\ell+3},\ldots
. This makes it clear that any
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
satisfies

f=\lim\limits_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}f\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)

(28)

(where the limit is taken with respect to the usual topology on
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
).

Let
\ell\left(\mu\right)
denote the number of parts of
\mu
. Fix an

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell\geq\ell\left(\mu\right)
. We shall
show that

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right).

(29)

Once this is done, the usual “let
\ell
tend to
\infty
” argument (analogous to
[
GriRei20
, proof of Corollary
2.6.11]
) will yield the validity of Theorem
2.16
.

Let
P_{\ell}
denote the set of all partitions with at most
\ell
parts. Any
partition
\lambda\in P_{\ell}
satisfies
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\right)
, and thus can be regarded as an

\ell
-tuple of nonnegative integers. In other words,
P_{\ell}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
. More precisely, the partitions
\lambda\in P_{\ell}
are
precisely the
\ell
-tuples
\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
satisfying
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{\ell}
.

Define the
\ell
-tuple
\rho=\left(\ell-1,\ell-2,\ldots,0\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.

For any
\ell
-tuple
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
and each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\right\}
, we shall write
\alpha_{i}
for the
i
-th entry
of
\alpha
.

For any
\ell
-tuple
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we let
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
denote the monomial
x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots x_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
denote the symmetric group of the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\right\}
. This group
\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
acts by

\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms on the polynomial ring
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]
. It also acts on the set
\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
by permuting the entries of an
\ell
-tuple; namely,

\sigma\cdot\beta=\left(\beta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(1\right)},\beta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(2\right)},\ldots,\beta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(\ell\right)}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}\text{ and }\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}.

For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, we let
\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}

denote the sign of the permutation
\sigma
.

If
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
is any
\ell
-tuple, then we define the
polynomial
a_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]
(called the
\alpha
-alternant
) by

a_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)=\det\left(\left(x_{j}^{\alpha_{i}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right).

We define addition of
\ell
-tuples
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
entrywise (so
that
\left(\alpha+\beta\right)_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}
for every

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
and
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\right\}
). Thus,
\lambda+\rho\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
for each
\lambda\in P_{\ell}

(since
\lambda\in P_{\ell}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
).

It is known (
[
GriRei20
, Corollary 2.6.7]
) that

s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)=\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}

(30)

for every
\lambda\in P_{\ell}
.

The partition
\mu
has at most
\ell
parts (since
\ell\geq\ell\left(\mu\right)
). In other words,
\mu\in P_{\ell}
.

Now, define
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
by
\alpha=\mu+\rho
. Proposition

2.3

(b)
yields

G\left(k\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(x_{i}^{0}+x_{i}^{1}+\cdots+x_{i}^{k-1}\right).

Substituting
0,0,0,\ldots
for
x_{\ell+1},x_{\ell+2},x_{\ell+3},\ldots
in
this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(x_{i}^{0}+x_{i}^{1}+\cdots+x_{i}^{k-1}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\prod\limits_{i=\ell+1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\left(0^{0}+0^{1}+\cdots+0^{k-1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }0^{0}=1\text{ and }0^{j}=0\text{ for all
}j>0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(x_{i}^{0}+x_{i}^{1}+\cdots+x_{i}^{k-1}\right)

(31)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{\ell}\right)\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{\ell}}x_{1}^{j_{1}}x_{2}^{j_{2}}\cdots x_{\ell}^{j_{\ell}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the product rule}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\beta_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}

(32)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have substituted }\beta\text{
for }\left(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{\ell}\right)\text{ in the sum}\right).

(Here and in the rest of this proof, “for all
i
” means “for all
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\right\}
”.)

From (
31
), we see that
\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)
is a
polynomial in
\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]
(not
merely a power series). From (
31
), we moreover
see that this polynomial
\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]
is invariant under the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
.

But from
a_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot a_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\underbrace{\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sigma\left(\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\right)\\
\text{(since the polynomial }\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\\
\text{is invariant under the action of
}\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\underbrace{\sigma\left(\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\right)\cdot\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sigma\left(\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)\\
\text{(since }\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}\text{ acts on }\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right]\\
\text{by }\mathbf{k}\text{-algebra
homomorphisms)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\beta_{i}<k\text{
for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.Gkxl2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\beta_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{x}^{\beta}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{\beta}\\
=\mathbf{x}^{\alpha+\beta}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\beta_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\sigma\left(\mathbf{x}^{\alpha+\beta}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=a_{\alpha+\beta}\\
\text{(by the definition of }a_{\alpha+\beta}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\beta_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha+\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}

(33)

(here, we have substituted
\gamma
for
\alpha+\beta
in the sum).

It is well-known (and easy to check using the properties of
determinants
19
19

19

specifically: using the fact that a square matrix with
two equal rows always has determinant
0
) that if an
\ell
-tuple
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
has two equal entries, then

a_{\gamma}=0.

(34)

Moreover, any
\ell
-tuple
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
and any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
satisfy

a_{\sigma\cdot\gamma}=\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\cdot a_{\gamma}.

(35)

(This, too, follows from the properties of determinants
20
20

20

specifically:
using the fact that permuting the rows of a square matrix results in its
determinant getting multiplied by the sign of the permutation
.)

Let
SP_{\ell}
denote the set of all
\ell
-tuples
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
such that
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\cdots>\delta_{\ell}
. Then, the map

\displaystyle P_{\ell}

\displaystyle\rightarrow SP_{\ell},

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda+\rho

(36)

is a bijection.

If an
\ell
-tuple
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
has no two equal entries, then

\gamma
can be uniquely written in the form
\sigma\cdot\delta
for some

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
and some
\delta\in SP_{\ell}
(indeed,

\delta
is the result of sorting
\gamma
into decreasing order, while

\sigma
is the permutation that achieves this sorting). In other words, the
map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}\times SP_{\ell}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\ \mid\ \text{the }\ell\text{-tuple }\gamma\text{ has no
two equal entries}\right\},

\displaystyle\left(\sigma,\delta\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma\cdot\delta

(37)

is a bijection.

Now, (
33
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot a_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i;\\
\text{the }\ell\text{-tuple }\gamma\text{ has no two
equal entries}\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i;\\
\text{the }\ell\text{-tuple
}\gamma\text{ has two equal entries}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{a_{\gamma}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.agam=0}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i;\\
\text{the }\ell\text{-tuple }\gamma\text{ has no two
equal entries}\end{subarray}}a_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell};\\
\text{the }\ell\text{-tuple
}\gamma\text{ has no two equal entries}\end{subarray}}\left[0\leq\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]\cdot a_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\left(\sigma,\delta\right)\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}\times SP_{\ell}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\delta\in SP_{\ell}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}}\left[0\leq\underbrace{\left(\sigma\cdot\delta\right)_{i}}_{=\delta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(i\right)}}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]\cdot\underbrace{a_{\sigma\cdot\delta}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}a_{\delta}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.asiggam=}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\sigma\cdot\delta\text{ for }\gamma\text{ in
the sum,}\\
\text{since the map (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.SSPbbij}) is a bijection}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\delta\in SP_{\ell}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\underbrace{\left[0\leq\delta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(i\right)}-\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left[0\leq\delta_{\sigma^{-1}\left(i\right)}-\alpha_{i}<k\right]\\
=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left[0\leq\delta_{i}-\alpha_{\sigma\left(i\right)}<k\right]\\
\text{(here, we have substituted }\sigma\left(i\right)\\
\text{for }i\text{ in the product, since }\sigma\text{ is a bijection)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}a_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\delta\in SP_{\ell}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left[0\leq\delta_{i}-\alpha_{\sigma\left(i\right)}<k\right]\right)\cdot\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}}\left(-1\right)^{\sigma}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\left[0\leq\delta_{i}-\alpha_{\sigma\left(i\right)}<k\right]\\
=\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\delta_{i}-\alpha_{j}<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)\\
\text{(by the definition of a determinant)}\end{subarray}}a_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\delta\in SP_{\ell}}\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\delta_{i}-\alpha_{j}<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)a_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in P_{\ell}}\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}-\alpha_{j}<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)a_{\lambda+\rho}

(40)

(here, we have substituted
\lambda+\rho
for
\delta
in the sum, since the
map (
36
) is a bijection).

Using the definitions of
\rho
and
\alpha
, it is easy to see that

\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}-\alpha_{j}=\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j

(41)

for every
\lambda\in P_{\ell}
and every
i,j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\right\}
.

Now, (
30
) (applied to
\lambda=\mu
) yields

s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)=\dfrac{a_{\mu+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=\dfrac{a_{\alpha}}{a_{\rho}}

(since
\mu+\rho=\alpha
). Multiplying this equality by
\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)
, we find

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot s_{\mu}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot\dfrac{a_{\alpha}}{a_{\rho}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{a_{\rho}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(G\left(k\right)\right)\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\cdot a_{\alpha}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in P_{\ell}}\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}-\alpha_{j}<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)a_{\lambda+\rho}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.prod2}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in P_{\ell}}\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\underbrace{\left(\lambda+\rho\right)_{i}-\alpha_{j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.dif}))}\end{subarray}}<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)\underbrace{\dfrac{a_{\lambda+\rho}}{a_{\rho}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.pieri.short.slam}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in P_{\ell}}\underbrace{\det\left(\left(\left[0\leq\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j<k\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\\
\text{(by the definition of }\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in P_{\ell}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right),

where the last equality sign is owed to the well-known fact (see, e.g.,

[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.3.8(b)]
) that every
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\setminus P_{\ell}
(that is, every partition
\lambda
having more than

\ell
parts) satisfies
s_{\lambda}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\right)=0
. Thus, (
29
) has been proved.

Forget that we fixed
\ell
. Thus, we have proved
(
29
) for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfies

\ell\geq\ell\left(\mu\right)
. As we mentioned above, we can now use a
standard limiting argument (using (
28
)) to obtain
the claim of Theorem
2.16
.

3.10
Proofs of Corollary

2.17
, Theorem
2.8
and Corollary
2.9

Having proved Theorem
2.16
, we can now obtain Corollary

2.17
, Theorem
2.8
and Corollary
2.9

as easy consequences:

Proof 3.19

(Proof of Corollary
2.17
.)
.

Proposition
2.3

(a)
yields that the
m
-th degree homogeneous component of
G\left(k\right)
is
G\left(k,m\right)
. Hence, the
\left(m+\left|\mu\right|\right)
-th degree homogeneous component of
G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\mu}
is
G\left(k,m\right)\cdot s_{\mu}
(because

s_{\mu}
is homogeneous of degree
\left|\mu\right|
).

Theorem
2.16
yields

G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}.

Taking the
\left(m+\left|\mu\right|\right)
-th degree
homogeneous components on both sides of this equality, we obtain

G\left(k,m\right)\cdot s_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{m+\left|\mu\right|}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}

(because each Schur function
s_{\lambda}
is homogeneous of degree

\left|\lambda\right|
, whereas the
\left(m+\left|\mu\right|\right)
-th degree homogeneous component of
G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\mu}
is
G\left(k,m\right)\cdot s_{\mu}
). This proves
Corollary
2.17
.

Proof 3.20

(Proof of Theorem
2.8
.)
.

Theorem
2.16
(applied to

\mu=\varnothing
) yields

G\left(k\right)\cdot s_{\varnothing}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

Comparing this with
G\left(k\right)\cdot\underbrace{s_{\varnothing}}_{=1}=G\left(k\right)
, we obtain

G\left(k\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

This proves Theorem
2.8
.

Proof 3.21

(Proof of Corollary
2.9
.)
.

Corollary
2.17
(applied
to
\mu=\varnothing
) yields

G\left(k,m\right)\cdot s_{\varnothing}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{m+\left|\varnothing\right|}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

In view of
G\left(k,m\right)\cdot\underbrace{s_{\varnothing}}_{=1}=G\left(k,m\right)
and
m+\underbrace{\left|\varnothing\right|}_{=0}=m
, we can rewrite this as

G\left(k,m\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{m}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

This proves Corollary
2.9
.

3.11
Proof of Theorem

2.14

Our proof of Theorem
2.14
will depend on two lemmas about determinants:

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
R
be a commutative
ring. Let
\left(a_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq m}\in R^{m\times m}
be an
m\times m
-matrix.

(a)
If
\tau
is any permutation of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,m\right\}
, then

\det\left(\left(a_{\tau\left(i\right),j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq m}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{\tau}\cdot\det\left(\left(a_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq m}\right).

Here,
\left(-1\right)^{\tau}
denotes the sign of the permutation
\tau
.

(b)
Let
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{m}
be
m
elements of
R
. Let

v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m}
be
m
elements of
R
. Then,

\det\left(\left(u_{i}v_{j}a_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq m}\right)=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\left(u_{i}v_{i}\right)\right)\cdot\det\left(\left(a_{i,j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq m}\right).

Proof 3.23

(Proof of Lemma
3.22
.)
.

(a)
This is just the
well-known fact that if the rows of a square matrix are permuted using a
permutation
\tau
, then the determinant of this matrix gets multiplied by

\left(-1\right)^{\tau}
.

(b)
This follows easily from the definition of the determinant.

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
k
be a positive integer. Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
be
k-1
elements of the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
.

Let
G
be the
\left(k-1\right)\times\left(k-1\right)
-matrix

\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}.

(a)
If the
k-1
numbers
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}

are not distinct, then

\det G=0.

(b)
If
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2}>\cdots>\gamma_{k-1}
, then

\det G=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}.

(c)
Assume that the
k-1
numbers
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are distinct. Let

g=\left|\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{2}\ \mid\ i<j\text{ and }\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}\right\}\right|.

Then,

\det G=\left(-1\right)^{g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}.

Proof 3.25

(Proof of Lemma
3.24
.)
.

(a)
Assume that the
k-1

numbers
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are not distinct. In other
words, there exist two elements
u
and
v
of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
such that
u<v
and
\gamma_{u}=\gamma_{v}
. Consider these

u
and
v
. Now, from
\gamma_{u}=\gamma_{v}
, we conclude that the
u
-th
and the
v
-th rows of the matrix
G
are equal (by the construction of
G
).
Hence, the matrix
G
has two equal rows (since
u<v
). Thus,
\det G=0
. This
proves Lemma
3.24

(a)
.

(b)
Assume that
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2}>\cdots>\gamma_{k-1}
. Thus,

\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are distinct. Hence,
\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right\}
is a
\left(k-1\right)
-element set. But
\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right\}
is a subset of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
(since

\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are elements of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
). Therefore,
\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right\}
is a
\left(k-1\right)
-element subset of

\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
.

Hence,
\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right\}=\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}\setminus\left\{u\right\}
for some
u\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
(since any
\left(k-1\right)
-element subset of

\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
has such a form). Consider this
u
. From

\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right\}=\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}\setminus\left\{u\right\}
, we conclude that

\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are the
k-1
elements of the set

\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}\setminus\left\{u\right\}
, listed in
decreasing order (since
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2}>\cdots>\gamma_{k-1}
). In other
words,

\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right)=\left(k,k-1,\ldots,\widehat{u},\ldots,2,1\right),

(42)

where the “hat” over the
u
signifies that

u
is omitted from the list (i.e., the expression “
\left(k,k-1,\ldots,\widehat{u},\ldots,2,1\right)
” is understood
to mean the
\left(k-1\right)
-element list
\left(k,k-1,\ldots,u+1,u-1,\ldots,2,1\right)
, which contains all
k
integers from
1
to
k

in decreasing order except for
u
).

Now, we claim that

\displaystyle\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}\left[\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\right]

(43)

for any
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
.

[
Proof of (
43
):
Let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
. We must
prove the equality (
43
).

From
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we obtain
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
thus
k-1\geq 1
. Thus,
k>k-1\geq 1
. Hence,
k+1<2k
, so that
\left(k+1\right)\%k=1
.

If we don’t have
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
,
then both truth values

\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]
and
\left[\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\right]
are
0
(indeed, the statement “
\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}
” is false, since
otherwise it would imply
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
), and therefore the equality (
43
)
simplifies to
\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}0=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}0
in this case, which is obviously true. Hence,
for the rest of this proof, we WLOG assume that we do have
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

Adding
\gamma_{i}\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
with
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we find
\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,2k-1\right\}
. But if
p\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,2k-1\right\}
satisfies

p\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, then
p\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}
(since
the only numbers in
\left\{2,3,\ldots,2k-1\right\}
that leave the
remainders
0
and
1
upon division by
k
are
k
and
k+1
). Applying this
to
p=\gamma_{i}+j
, we obtain
\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}

(since
\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,2k-1\right\}
and
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
). Hence,
k\leq\gamma_{i}+j<2k
(since
k+1<2k
), so that
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)//k=1
. But
every integer
n
satisfies
n=\left(n//k\right)k+\left(n\%k\right)
.
Applying this to
n=\gamma_{i}+j
, we obtain
\gamma_{i}+j=\underbrace{\left(\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)//k\right)}_{=1}k+\left(\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\right)=k+\left(\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\right)
. Hence,
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k=\gamma_{i}+j-k
. Thus,

\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}\\
\text{(since }\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k=\gamma_{i}+j-k\text{)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}.

Comparing this with

\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}\underbrace{\left[\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k},

we obtain

\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}\left[\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\right].

This proves (
43
).]

Now,
G
is a
\left(k-1\right)\times\left(k-1\right)
-matrix. For
each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\text{the }\left(i,j\right)\text{-th entry of }G\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }G\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\gamma_{i}+j-k}\left[\gamma_{i}+j\in\left\{k,k+1\right\}\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.b.5})}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\gamma_{i}+j=k;\\
-1,&\text{if }\gamma_{i}+j=k+1;\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }j=k-\gamma_{i};\\
-1,&\text{if }j=k-\gamma_{i}+1;\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\ \ \ .

Thus, we can explicitly describe the matrix
G
as follows: For each

i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, the
i
-th row of
G
has an entry
equal to
1
in position
k-\gamma_{i}
if
k-\gamma_{i}>0
, and an entry
equal to
-1
in position
k-\gamma_{i}+1
if
k-\gamma_{i}+1<k
; all
remaining entries of this row are
0
. Recalling
(
42
), we thus see that
G
has the
following form:
21
21

21

Empty cells are understood to have entry
0
.

G=\left(\begin{tabular}[c]{ccccc|ccccc}$-1$&&&&&&&&&\\
$1$&$-1$&&&&&&&&\\
&$1$&$-1$&&&&&&&\\
&&$\ddots$&$\ddots$&&&&&&\\
&&&$1$&$-1$&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&$1$&$-1$&&&\\
&&&&&&$1$&$-1$&&\\
&&&&&&&$1$&$-1$&\\
&&&&&&&&$\ddots$&$\ddots$\\
&&&&&&&&&$1$\end{tabular}\right),

where the horizontal bar separates the
\left(k-u\right)
-th row from the

\left(k-u+1\right)
-st row, while the vertical bar separates the
\left(k-u\right)
-th column from the
\left(k-u+1\right)
-st column. Thus,
G

can be written as a block-diagonal matrix

G=\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}A&0_{\left(k-u\right)\times\left(u-1\right)}\\
0_{\left(u-1\right)\times\left(k-u\right)}&B\end{array}\right),

(44)

where
A
is a lower-triangular
\left(k-u\right)\times\left(k-u\right)
-matrix with all diagonal entries equal to
-1
, and where
B
is an
upper-triangular
\left(u-1\right)\times\left(u-1\right)
-matrix with
all diagonal entries equal to
1
. Since the determinant of a block-diagonal
matrix equals the product of the determinants of its diagonal blocks, we thus
conclude that

\displaystyle\det G

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\det A}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{k-u}\\
\text{(since }A\text{ is a}\\
\text{lower-triangular }\left(k-u\right)\times\left(k-u\right)\text{-matrix}\\
\text{with all diagonal entries
equal to }-1\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\det B}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since
}B\text{ is an}\\
\text{upper-triangular }\left(u-1\right)\times\left(u-1\right)\text{-matrix}\\
\text{with all diagonal entries equal to
}1\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{k-u}.

(45)

But (
42
) yields

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}

\displaystyle=k+\left(k-1\right)+\cdots+\widehat{u}+\cdots+2+1

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(k+\left(k-1\right)+\cdots+2+1\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1+2+\cdots+k\\
=\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)+k\end{subarray}}-u

\displaystyle=\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)+k-u.

Solving this for
k-u
, we find

k-u=\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right).

Hence, (
45
) rewrites as

\det G=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}.

This proves Lemma
3.24

(b)
.

(c)
Assume that the
k-1
numbers
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are distinct. Then, there exists a unique permutation

\sigma
of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
such that
\gamma_{\sigma\left(1\right)}>\gamma_{\sigma\left(2\right)}>\cdots>\gamma_{\sigma\left(k-1\right)}
(indeed, this is simply saying that the

\left(k-1\right)
-tuple
\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right)
can be sorted into decreasing order by a unique
permutation). Consider this
\sigma
.

Let
\tau
denote the permutation
\sigma^{-1}
. Thus,
\tau
is a permutation
of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and satisfies
\sigma\circ\tau=\operatorname*{id}
.

Let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{k-1}
denote the
k-1
elements

\gamma_{\sigma\left(1\right)},\gamma_{\sigma\left(2\right)},\ldots,\gamma_{\sigma\left(k-1\right)}
of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
. Thus, for each
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we have

\delta_{j}=\gamma_{\sigma\left(j\right)}.

(46)

Hence, the chain of inequalities
\gamma_{\sigma\left(1\right)}>\gamma_{\sigma\left(2\right)}>\cdots>\gamma_{\sigma\left(k-1\right)}

(which is true) can be rewritten as
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\cdots>\delta_{k-1}
.

Moreover, from (
46
), we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\cdots+\delta_{k-1}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\sigma\left(1\right)}+\gamma_{\sigma\left(2\right)}+\cdots+\gamma_{\sigma\left(k-1\right)}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}

(47)

(since
\sigma
is a permutation of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
).

Moreover, for each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\sigma\left(\tau\left(i\right)\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.c.1}), applied to }j=\tau\left(i\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\sigma\left(\tau\left(i\right)\right)=\underbrace{\left(\sigma\circ\tau\right)}_{=\operatorname*{id}}\left(i\right)=i\right).

(48)

Recall that an
inversion
of the permutation
\tau
is defined to be a
pair
\left(i,j\right)
of elements of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}

satisfying
i<j
and
\tau\left(i\right)>\tau\left(j\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\{\text{the inversions of }\tau\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{2}\ \mid\ i<j\text{ and }\underbrace{\tau\left(i\right)>\tau\left(j\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{This is equivalent to }\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}<\delta_{\tau\left(j\right)}\right)\\
\text{(since }\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\cdots>\delta_{k-1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{2}\ \mid\ i<j\text{ and }\underbrace{\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\gamma_{i}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.c.2}))}\end{subarray}}<\underbrace{\delta_{\tau\left(j\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\gamma_{j}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.c.2}))}\end{subarray}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{2}\ \mid\ i<j\text{ and }\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}\right\}.

(49)

Recall that the
length

\ell\left(\tau\right)
of the permutation

\tau
is defined to be the number of inversions of
\tau
. Thus,

\displaystyle\ell\left(\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the number of inversions of }\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{\text{the inversions of }\tau\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{2}\ \mid\ i<j\text{ and }\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}\right\}\right|\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.c.Inv})}\right)

\displaystyle=g\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }g\right).

Recall that the
sign

\left(-1\right)^{\tau}
of the permutation

\tau
is defined by
\left(-1\right)^{\tau}=\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\tau\right)}
. Hence,
\left(-1\right)^{\tau}=\left(-1\right)^{\ell\left(\tau\right)}=\left(-1\right)^{g}
(since

\ell\left(\tau\right)=g
).

Let
H
be the
\left(k-1\right)\times\left(k-1\right)
-matrix

\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}.

Then, we can apply Lemma
3.24

(b)
to
\delta_{i}
and
H
instead of
\gamma_{i}
and
G
(since
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots,\delta_{k-1}
are
k-1
elements of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
and satisfy
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\cdots>\delta_{k-1}
). We
thus obtain

\det H=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\cdots+\delta_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}

(by (
47
)).

But the definition of
G
yields

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\\
\text{(since
(\ref{pf.lem.petk.explicit.2.c.2}) yields }\gamma_{i}=\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\det G

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{\tau\left(i\right)}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\tau}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{g}}\det\left(\underbrace{\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=H\\
\text{(by the definition of }H\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.petk.explicit.1} {(a)}, applied to }m=k-1\text{
and }R=\mathbf{k}\\
\text{and }a_{i,j}=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\delta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{g}\underbrace{\det H}_{=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{g}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}.

This proves Lemma
3.24

(c)
.

Next, we recall a well-known property of symmetric functions:

Lemma 3.26
.

Consider the ring
\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of formal power series in one indeterminate
t
over

\Lambda
. In this ring, we have

1=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}t^{n}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right).

(50)

Lemma
3.26
is a well-known identity (see, e.g.,

[
Stanle01
, proof of Theorem 7.6.1]
or
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.3)]
); for the
sake of completeness, let us nevertheless give a proof:

Proof 3.27

(Proof of Lemma
3.26
.)
.

Consider the ring
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of formal power series in one
indeterminate
t
over
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
. In this ring, we have the equalities

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)^{-1}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}

(51)

and

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1+x_{i}t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}e_{n}t^{n}.

(52)

(Indeed, the first of these two equalities is
[
GriRei20
, (2.2.18)]
, whereas
the second is
[
GriRei20
, (2.2.19)]
.)

Substituting
-t
for
t
in the equality (
52
),
and multiplying the resulting equality by (
51
),
we obtain

\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)^{-1}\right)=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}e_{n}\left(-t\right)^{n}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right).

The left hand side of this equality simplifies to
1
, while the right hand
side is the right hand side of (
50
). Thus,
(
50
) holds, which proves Lemma

3.26
.

Next, we shall prove yet another evaluation of the homomorphism
\alpha_{k}
:

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
k
be a positive integer such that
k>1
. Consider
the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{k}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
from Definition
3.10
. Also, recall Convention

2.1
. Let
r
be an integer such that
r>-k+1
. Then,

\alpha_{k}\left(e_{r}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{r+r\%k}\left[r\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right].

(53)

Proof 3.29

(Proof of Lemma
3.28
.)
.

Consider the ring
\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of formal power series in one indeterminate
t
over

\Lambda
. Consider also the analogous ring
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
over
\mathbf{k}
.

The map
\alpha_{k}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism. Hence, it induces a continuous
22
22

22

Continuity is defined
with respect to the usual topologies on
\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
and
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
, where we equip
both
\Lambda
and
\mathbf{k}
with the discrete topologies.

\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism

\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]:\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]

that sends each formal power series
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(with
a_{n}\in\Lambda
) to
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\left(a_{n}\right)t^{n}
. Consider this
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

Applying the map
\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
to both sides
of the equality (
50
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(1\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}t^{n}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}t^{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\left(\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}\right)t^{n}\\
\text{(by the definition of }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\alpha_{k}\left(h_{n}\right)t^{n}\\
\text{(by
the definition of }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{ is a }\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{-algebra homomorphism}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\left(\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{n}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\\
\text{(since }\alpha_{k}\text{ is }\mathbf{k}\text{-linear)}\end{subarray}}t^{n}\right)\cdot\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[n<k\right]\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.G.main.alk}))}\end{subarray}}t^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left[n<k\right]t^{n}\right)}_{=t^{0}+t^{1}+\cdots+t^{k-1}=\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}\right)\cdot\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}.

Comparing this with

\left(\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(1\right)=1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\alpha_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{ is a }\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{-algebra homomorphism}\right),

we obtain

\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}\right)\cdot\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}=1.

Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1-t}{1-t^{k}}=\left(1-t\right)\cdot\underbrace{\dfrac{1}{1-t^{k}}}_{=1+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots}

\displaystyle=\left(1-t\right)\cdot\left(1+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=1-t+t^{k}-t^{k+1}+t^{2k}-t^{2k+1}+t^{3k}-t^{3k+1}\pm\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\left(-1\right)^{n\%k}\left[n\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]t^{n}

(here, we have used that
k>1
, since for
k=1
there would be cancellations
in the sum
1-t+t^{k}-t^{k+1}+t^{2k}-t^{2k+1}+t^{3k}-t^{3k+1}\pm\cdots
).
Comparing coefficients before
t^{m}
on both sides of this equality, we
obtain

\left(-1\right)^{m}\alpha_{k}\left(e_{m}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{m\%k}\left[m\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

(54)

for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Multiplying both sides of this equality by
\left(-1\right)^{m}
, we
obtain

\alpha_{k}\left(e_{m}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{m+m\%k}\left[m\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right].

(55)

We must prove that

\alpha_{k}\left(e_{r}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{r+r\%k}\left[r\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right].

If
r\in\mathbb{N}
, then this follows by applying
(
55
) to
m=r
. Hence, for the rest of
this proof, we WLOG assume that
r\notin\mathbb{N}
. Thus,
r
is negative, so
that
r\in\left\{-k+2,-k+3,\ldots,-1\right\}
(since
r>-k+1
). Hence,

r\%k\in\left\{2,3,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, so that
r\%k\notin\left\{0,1\right\}
. Consequently,
\left[r\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]=0
. Also,
e_{r}=0
(since
r
is negative) and thus
\alpha_{k}\left(e_{r}\right)=\alpha_{k}\left(0\right)=0
. Comparing this with
\left(-1\right)^{r+r\%k}\underbrace{\left[r\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]}_{=0}=0
, we obtain
\alpha_{k}\left(e_{r}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{r+r\%k}\left[r\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]
. This concludes the
proof of Lemma
3.28
.

Proof 3.30

(Proof of Theorem
2.14
.)
.

(a)
Assume that
\mu_{k}\neq 0
. But
\mu=\lambda^{t}
, whence

\mu_{k}=\left(\lambda^{t}\right)_{k}=\left|\left\{j\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\ \mid\ \lambda_{j}\geq k\right\}\right|\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }\lambda^{t}\right).

Hence,

\left|\left\{j\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\ \mid\ \lambda_{j}\geq k\right\}\right|=\mu_{k}\neq 0.

In other words, the set
\left\{j\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\ \mid\ \lambda_{j}\geq k\right\}
is nonempty. Hence, there exists some

j\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
satisfying
\lambda_{j}\geq k
. Consider
this
j
. We have
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq\cdots
(since

\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}
) and thus
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{j}
(since

1\leq j
). Hence,
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{j}\geq k
. Thus, Proposition

2.11
yields
\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)=0
. This proves Theorem
2.14

(a)
.

Now, let us prepare for the proof of parts
(b)
and
(c)
.

Consider the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{k}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
from Definition
3.10
.

For each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, we have
\left(\beta_{i}-1\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}
(by the definition of
a remainder) and thus
\gamma_{i}\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
(by
(
8
)). In other words,
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are
k-1
elements of the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}
.

Assume that
\mu_{k}=0
. Thus,
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{k-1}\right)
(since
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}
).

It is known that taking the transpose of the transpose of a partition returns
the original partition. Thus,
\left(\lambda^{t}\right)^{t}=\lambda
. In
view of
\mu=\lambda^{t}
, this rewrites as
\mu^{t}=\lambda
. Hence,

\lambda=\mu^{t}
. Therefore,

s_{\lambda}=s_{\mu^{t}}=\det\left(\left(e_{\mu_{i}-i+j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

(by (
6
), applied to
\mu
and
k-1
instead of
\lambda

and
\ell
), because
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{k-1}\right)
.
Applying the map
\alpha_{k}
to both sides of this equality, we find

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\left(s_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}\left(\det\left(\left(\underbrace{e_{\mu_{i}-i+j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=e_{\beta_{i}+j}\\
\text{(since
(\ref{eq.thm.petk.explicit.beti=}) yields }\mu_{i}-i=\beta_{i}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}\left(\det\left(\left(e_{\beta_{i}+j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\alpha_{k}\left(e_{\beta_{i}+j}\right)\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

(since
\alpha_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism, and thus commutes
with taking determinants of matrices). On the other hand,

\alpha_{k}\left(\underbrace{s_{\lambda}}_{=s_{\lambda/\varnothing}}\right)=\alpha_{k}\left(s_{\lambda/\varnothing}\right)=\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)

(by (
23
), applied to
\varnothing
instead of
\mu
).
Comparing these two equalities, we obtain

\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)=\det\left(\left(\alpha_{k}\left(e_{\beta_{i}+j}\right)\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right).

(56)

But each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
satisfy
k>1

23
23

23

Indeed, if

i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
, then
1\leq i\leq k-1
and thus

k-1\geq 1>0
, so that
k>1
.
and

\underbrace{\beta_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mu_{i}-i\\
\text{(by
(\ref{eq.thm.petk.explicit.beti=}))}\end{subarray}}+j=\underbrace{\mu_{i}}_{\geq 0}-\underbrace{i}_{\leq k-1}+\underbrace{j}_{>0}>0-\left(k-1\right)+0=-k+1

and thus

\alpha_{k}\left(e_{\beta_{i}+j}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

(57)

(by (
53
), applied to
r=\beta_{i}+j
).

Furthermore, each
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{j}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right].

(58)

[
Proof of (
58
):
Let
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
and
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\right\}
. The
definition of
\gamma_{i}
yields

\gamma_{i}=1+\underbrace{\left(\beta_{i}-1\right)\%k}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\equiv\beta_{i}-1\operatorname{mod}k\\
\text{(since }u\%k\equiv u\operatorname{mod}k\text{ for any }u\in\mathbb{Z}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\equiv 1+\left(\beta_{i}-1\right)=\beta_{i}\operatorname{mod}k.

Hence,
\underbrace{\gamma_{i}}_{\equiv\beta_{i}\operatorname{mod}k}+j\equiv\beta_{i}+j\operatorname{mod}k
. But if
u\in\mathbb{Z}
and

v\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
u\equiv v\operatorname{mod}k
, then
u\%k=v\%k
.
Applying this to
u=\gamma_{i}+j
and
v=\beta_{i}+j
, we obtain
\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k=\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{j}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{j}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right].

This proves (
58
).]

Now, (
56
) becomes

\displaystyle\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\underbrace{\alpha_{k}\left(e_{\beta_{i}+j}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.petk.explicit.akem1a}))}\end{subarray}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)+\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\beta_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{j}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.petk.explicit.akem2}))}\end{subarray}}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{j}\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)\right)\cdot\det\left(\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}\right)

(by Lemma
3.22

(b)
, applied to
m=k-1
and

R=\mathbf{k}
and

a_{i,j}=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]
and
u_{i}=\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}
and

v_{j}=\left(-1\right)^{j}
).

Define a
\left(k-1\right)\times\left(k-1\right)
-matrix
G
as in
Lemma
3.24
. Then, this becomes

\displaystyle\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)\right)\cdot\det\left(\underbrace{\left(\left(-1\right)^{\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k}\left[\left(\gamma_{i}+j\right)\%k\in\left\{0,1\right\}\right]\right)_{1\leq i\leq k-1,\ 1\leq j\leq k-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=G\\
\text{(by the definition of }G\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)\right)\cdot\det G.

(59)

Now, we can readily prove parts
(b)
and
(c)
of Theorem

2.14
:

(b)
Assume that the
k-1
numbers
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}
are not distinct. Then, Lemma
3.24

(a)
yields
\det G=0
. Hence, (
59
)
yields

\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)\right)\cdot\underbrace{\det G}_{=0}=0.

This proves Theorem
2.14

(b)
.

(c)
The equality (
59
) becomes

\displaystyle\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)\right)}_{=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)}\cdot\underbrace{\det G}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.petk.explicit.2} {(c)})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{i}}\right)}_{=\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{k-1}}}\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)}_{=\left(-1\right)^{1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)}}\cdot\left(-1\right)^{g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{k-1}}\left(-1\right)^{1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)}\cdot\left(-1\right)^{g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{k-1}\right)+\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)+g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)-\left(1+2+\cdots+\left(k-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{\left(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\cdots+\beta_{k-1}\right)+g+\left(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{k-1}\right)}.

This proves Theorem
2.14

(c)
.

The proof of Proposition
2.15
relies on the following
known fact:

Proposition 3.31
.

Let
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}
. Let

\mu=\lambda^{t}
. Then:

(a)
If
i
and
j
are two positive integers satisfying
\lambda_{i}\geq j
, then
\mu_{j}\geq i
.

(b)
If
i
and
j
are two positive integers satisfying
\lambda_{i}<j
, then
\mu_{j}<i
.

(c)
Any two positive integers
i
and
j
satisfy
\lambda_{i}+\mu_{j}-i-j\neq-1
.

For each positive integer
i
, set
\alpha_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
. For each
positive integer
j
, set
\beta_{j}=\mu_{j}-j
and
\eta_{j}=-1-\beta_{j}
. Then:

(d)
The two sets
\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right\}
and
\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\ldots\right\}

are disjoint, and their union is
\mathbb{Z}
.

(e)
Let
p
be an integer such that
p\geq\lambda_{1}
. Then, the two
sets
\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right\}
and

\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{p}\right\}
are disjoint, and their
union is

\left\{\ldots,p-3,p-2,p-1\right\}=\left\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\ \mid\ k<p\right\}.

(f)
Let
p
and
q
be two integers such that
p\geq\lambda_{1}
and

q\geq\mu_{1}
. Then, the two sets
\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}\right\}
and
\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{p}\right\}
are disjoint, and their union is

\left\{-q,-q+1,\ldots,p-1\right\}=\left\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\ \mid\ -q\leq k<p\right\}.

Note that Proposition
3.31

(f)
is a
restatement of
[
Macdon95
, Chapter I, (1.7)]
.

Proof 3.32

(Proof of Proposition
3.31
(sketched).)
.

Left to
the reader (see
[
Grinbe20b
]
for a detailed proof). The easiest way to
proceed is by proving
(a)
and
(b)
first, then deriving

(c)
as their consequence, then deriving
(f)
from it, then
concluding
(d)
and
(e)
.

Proof 3.33

(Proof of Proposition
2.15
(sketched).)
.

Let

\mu=\lambda^{t}
. Then, the number of parts of
\mu
is
\lambda_{1}
. Hence,
from
\lambda_{1}<k
, we conclude that
\mu
has fewer than
k
parts. Thus,

\mu_{k}=0
.

For each positive integer
i
, set
\alpha_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\underbrace{\alpha_{i}}_{=\lambda_{i}-i}\ \mid\ i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\right\}=\left\{\lambda_{i}-i\ \mid\ i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=B\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }B\right).

For each positive integer
j
, set
\beta_{j}=\mu_{j}-j
and
\eta_{j}=-1-\beta_{j}
. Note that
\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}
is thus the same
\left(k-1\right)
-tuple that was called
\left(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}\right)
in Theorem
2.14
. It is easy to see that

\beta_{1}>\beta_{2}>\cdots>\beta_{k-1}
and
\lambda_{1}-1>\lambda_{2}-2>\lambda_{3}-3>\cdots
.

From
\lambda_{1}<k
, we obtain
k-1\geq\lambda_{1}
. Hence, Proposition

3.31

(e)
(applied to
p=k-1
) yields
that the two sets
\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right\}

and
\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}
are disjoint, and
their union is

\left\{\ldots,\left(k-1\right)-3,\left(k-1\right)-2,\left(k-1\right)-1\right\}=\left\{\text{all integers smaller than
}k-1\right\}=W.

Since
\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right\}=B
, we can
restate this as follows: The two sets
B
and
\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}
are disjoint, and their union is
W
. Hence,

\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}=W\setminus B
.

It is also easy to see that
\beta_{1}>\beta_{2}>\cdots>\beta_{k-1}
, so that

\eta_{1}<\eta_{2}<\cdots<\eta_{k-1}
. Hence,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}
are the elements of the set
\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}
listed in increasing order (with no repetition).

Let us define a
\left(k-1\right)
-tuple
\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\right)\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,k\right\}^{k-1}
as
in Theorem
2.14
. Now, we have the following chain of
logical equivalences:

\displaystyle\ \left(\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)\neq 0\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{the }k-1\text{ numbers }\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1}\text{ are distinct}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by parts {(b)}
and {(c)} of Theorem \ref{thm.petk.explicit}}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{the }k-1\text{ numbers }\left(\beta_{1}-1\right)\%k,\left(\beta_{2}-1\right)\%k,\ldots,\left(\beta_{k-1}-1\right)\%k\text{ are distinct}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\gamma_{i}=1+\left(\beta_{i}-1\right)\%k\text{ for each }i\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ numbers }\beta_{1}-1,\beta_{2}-1,\ldots,\beta_{k-1}-1\text{ are congruent modulo
}k\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ numbers }\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}\text{ are congruent modulo }k\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ numbers
}-1-\beta_{1},-1-\beta_{2},\ldots,-1-\beta_{k-1}\text{ }\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{are congruent modulo }k\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ numbers }\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\text{ are congruent modulo }k\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\eta_{j}=-1-\beta_{j}\text{ for each }j\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ elements of
}\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}\text{ are congruent
modulo }k\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\text{ are the elements of the
set }\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}\\
\text{listed in increasing order (with no repetition)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no two of the }k-1\text{ elements of
}W\setminus B\text{ are congruent modulo }k\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\left\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\right\}=W\setminus B\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{each congruence class }\overline{i}\text{ has at most }1\text{ element in common with }W\setminus B\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{each }i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}\text{ satisfies }\left|\overline{i}\cap\left(W\setminus B\right)\right|\leq 1\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{each }i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}\text{ satisfies }\left|\left(\overline{i}\cap W\right)\setminus B\right|\leq 1\right)

(since
\overline{i}\cap\left(W\setminus B\right)=\left(\overline{i}\cap W\right)\setminus B
for each
i
). This proves Proposition

2.15
.
