Proof of Theorem
2.18

Proof 3.34

(Proof of Theorem
2.18
.)
.

In this proof, the word
“monomial” may refer to a monomial in any
set of variables (not necessarily in
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
).

In the following, an
i
-monomial
(where
i\in\mathbb{N}
) shall mean a
monomial of degree
i
.

We shall say that a monomial is
k
-bounded
if all exponents in this
monomial are
<k
. In other words, a monomial is
k
-bounded
if it can
be written in the form
z_{1}^{a_{1}}z_{2}^{a_{2}}\cdots z_{s}^{a_{s}}
, where

z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{s}
are distinct variables and
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{s}
are nonnegative integers
<k
. Thus, the
k
-bounded monomials
in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
are precisely the monomials of the
form
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}
satisfying

\left(\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right)
. Hence, the
k
-bounded

m
-monomials in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
are precisely the
monomials of the form
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}

satisfying
\left|\alpha\right|=m
and
\left(\alpha_{i}<k\text{
for all }i\right)
.

Now, the definition of
G\left(k,m\right)
yields

\displaystyle G\left(k,m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m;\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the
variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right)

(60)

(since the
k
-bounded
m
-monomials in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
are precisely the monomials of the form
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

for
\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}
satisfying
\left|\alpha\right|=m

and
\left(\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right)
).

Let us now substitute the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
for the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
on both
sides of the equality (
60
). (This means that we choose
some bijection
\phi:\left\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right\}\rightarrow\left\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right\}
, and
substitute
\phi\left(x_{i}\right)
for each
x_{i}
on both sides of
(
60
).) The left hand side of
(
60
) turns into
\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)
upon this
substitution
24
24

24

because this is how
\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)
was defined
, whereas the
right hand side turns into

\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the
variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right)

25
25

25

Indeed, the substitution can be regarded as simply renaming the
variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
as
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
(in some order). Thus, it turns the
k
-bounded

m
-monomials in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
into the
k
-bounded

m
-monomials in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
.

. Thus, our substitution transforms the equality
(
60
) into

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the
variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right).

(61)

But any monomial
\mathfrak{m}
in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
can be uniquely written as a product

\mathfrak{np}
, where
\mathfrak{n}
is a monomial in the variables

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
and where
\mathfrak{p}
is a monomial in the
variables
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{m}
is written
in this form, then:

•

the degree of
\mathfrak{m}
equals the sum of the degrees of

\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{p}
;

•

thus,
\mathfrak{m}
is an
m
-monomial if and only if there exists some

i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
such that
\mathfrak{n}
is an

i
-monomial and
\mathfrak{p}
is an
\left(m-i\right)
-monomial;

•

furthermore,
\mathfrak{m}
is
k
-bounded if and only if both

\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{p}
are
k
-bounded.

Thus, any
k
-bounded
m
-monomial
\mathfrak{m}
in the variables

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
can be uniquely written as
a product
\mathfrak{np}
, where
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
, where

\mathfrak{n}
is a
k
-bounded
i
-monomial in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
and where
\mathfrak{p}
is a
k
-bounded
\left(m-i\right)
-monomial in the variables
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
.
Conversely, every such product
\mathfrak{np}
is a
k
-bounded
m
-monomial

\mathfrak{m}
in the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
. Thus, we obtain a bijection

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\left(\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{k\text{-bounded }i\text{-monomials in the variables
}x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left\{k\text{-bounded }\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomials in the variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the variables
}x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right\}

that sends each pair
\left(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{p}\right)
to

\mathfrak{np}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the
variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{n}\text{ is a }k\text{-bounded}\\
i\text{-monomial in the}\\
\text{variables
}x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\text{ is a
}k\text{-bounded}\\
\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomial in the}\\
\text{variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{np}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{n}\text{ is a }k\text{-bounded}\\
i\text{-monomial in
the}\\
\text{variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{n}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\text{ is a }k\text{-bounded}\\
\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomial in the}\\
\text{variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\left(\text{the sum of all
}k\text{-bounded }i\text{-monomials in the variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded
}\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomials in the variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right).

(62)

Now, let
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
. The same reasoning that gave
us (
60
) can be applied to
i
instead of
m
. Thus we
obtain

G\left(k,i\right)=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded
}i\text{-monomials in the variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right).

(63)

Also,
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
, so that
m-i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. Hence, the same reasoning that
gave us (
60
) can be applied to
m-i
instead of
m
.
Thus we obtain

\displaystyle G\left(k,m-i\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomials in the variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right).

Renaming the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
as
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
in this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k,m-i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomials in the variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right).

(64)

Forget that we fixed
i
. We thus have proved (
63
) and
(
64
) for each
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
.

Now, (
61
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{the sum of all }k\text{-bounded }m\text{-monomials in the
variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\underbrace{\left(\text{the
sum of all }k\text{-bounded }i\text{-monomials in the variables }x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=G\left(k,i\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.DeltaGkm.Gki=}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\underbrace{\left(\text{the sum of all
}k\text{-bounded }\left(m-i\right)\text{-monomials in the variables }y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(G\left(k,m-i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.DeltaGkm.Gkm-iy=}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.DeltaGkm.prod1})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\underbrace{G\left(k,i\right)}_{=\left(G\left(k,i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}\cdot\left(G\left(k,m-i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{y}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}\left(G\left(k,i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\cdot\left(G\left(k,m-i\right)\right)\left(\mathbf{y}\right).

Hence, (
10
) holds for
f=G\left(k,m\right)
,

I=\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
,
\left(f_{1,i}\right)_{i\in I}=\left(G\left(k,i\right)\right)_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}
and
\left(f_{2,i}\right)_{i\in I}=\left(G\left(k,m-i\right)\right)_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}
. Therefore,
(
9
) (applied to these
f
,
I
,
\left(f_{1,i}\right)_{i\in I}
and
\left(f_{2,i}\right)_{i\in I}
) yields

\Delta\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}G\left(k,i\right)\otimes G\left(k,m-i\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}G\left(k,i\right)\otimes G\left(k,m-i\right).

This proves Theorem
2.18
.

3.13
Proof of Theorem
2.20

Proof 3.35

(Proof of Theorem
2.20
.)
.

Consider the ring
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of formal power series in one indeterminate
t

over
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
.
We equip this ring with the topology that is obtained by identifying it with

\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,t\right]\right]
(or,
equivalently, which is obtained by considering
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
itself as equipped with the
standard topology on a ring of formal power series, and then adjoining the
extra indeterminate
t
).

Now, consider the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{k}:\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right],

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto a\left(x_{1}^{k},x_{2}^{k},x_{3}^{k},\ldots\right).

This map
\mathbf{F}_{k}
is a continuous
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
(since it is an evaluation homomorphism)
26
26

26

It is well-defined, since

k
is positive.
. Hence, it induces a continuous
27
27

27

Continuity is
defined with respect to the topology that we defined on
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism

\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]:\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]\rightarrow\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]

that sends each formal power series
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}\in\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(with
a_{n}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
) to
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(a_{n}\right)t^{n}
. Consider this
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism
\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
. In particular, it satisfies

\left(\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(t^{i}\right)=t^{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\in\mathbb{N}.

The definition of
\mathbf{F}_{k}
yields

\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(x_{i}\right)=x_{i}^{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
each }i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}.

(65)

Also, for each
a\in\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(a\right)

\displaystyle=a\left(x_{1}^{k},x_{2}^{k},x_{3}^{k},\ldots\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}\mathbf{F}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(a\right)

(66)

(since the definition of
\mathbf{f}_{k}
yields
\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(a\right)=a\left(x_{1}^{k},x_{2}^{k},x_{3}^{k},\ldots\right)
). Thus, in
particular, each
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)

(67)

(by (
66
), applied to
a=e_{n}
).

Applying the map
\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
to both
sides of the equality (
52
), we obtain

\left(\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1+x_{i}t\right)\right)=\left(\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}e_{n}t^{n}\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}

(by the definition of
\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
).
Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1+x_{i}t\right)\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\left(\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(1+x_{i}t\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1+\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(x_{i}\right)t\\
\text{(by the definition of }\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }\mathbf{F}_{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{ is a
continuous }\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{-algebra
homomorphism,}\\
\text{and thus respects infinite products}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\underbrace{\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(x_{i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{i}^{k}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.G.frob.Fkxi}))}\end{subarray}}t\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1+x_{i}^{k}t\right).

Substituting
-t^{k}
for
t
in this equality, we find

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-t^{k}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\left(1+x_{i}^{k}\left(-t^{k}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1-\left(x_{i}t\right)^{k}\\
=\left(1-x_{i}t\right)\left(\left(x_{i}t\right)^{0}+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{1}+\cdots+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{k-1}\right)\\
\text{(since }1-u^{k}=\left(1-u\right)\left(u^{0}+u^{1}+\cdots+u^{k-1}\right)\\
\text{for any element }u\text{ of any ring)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\left(1-x_{i}t\right)\left(\left(x_{i}t\right)^{0}+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{1}+\cdots+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{k-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)\right)\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\left(x_{i}t\right)^{0}+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{1}+\cdots+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{k-1}\right)\right).

We can divide both sides of this equality by
\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)
(since the formal power series
\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)
has constant term
1
and thus is invertible), and thus
obtain

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-t^{k}\right)^{n}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\left(\left(x_{i}t\right)^{0}+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{1}+\cdots+\left(x_{i}t\right)^{k-1}\right)}_{=\sum\limits_{u=0}^{k-1}\left(x_{i}t\right)^{u}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\ \ \sum\limits_{u=0}^{k-1}\left(x_{i}t\right)^{u}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots\right)\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{\infty};\\
\alpha_{i}=0\text{ for all but finitely many }i\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\right\}^{\infty}\\
\text{is a
weak composition}\end{subarray}}\\
=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\left(x_{1}t\right)^{\alpha_{1}}\left(x_{2}t\right)^{\alpha_{2}}\left(x_{3}t\right)^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots\right)\left(t^{\alpha_{1}}t^{\alpha_{2}}t^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots\right)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have
expanded the product}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\left(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}x_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\left(t^{\alpha_{1}}t^{\alpha_{2}}t^{\alpha_{3}}\cdots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\cdots}=t^{\left|\alpha\right|}\\
\text{(since }\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\cdots=\left|\alpha\right|\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}t^{\left|\alpha\right|}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}t^{\left|\alpha\right|}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-t^{k}\right)^{n}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)}=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\underbrace{\mathbf{F}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.G.frob.Fken}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\left(-t^{k}\right)^{n}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{n}t^{kn}}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)^{-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.h-e-reciprocity-t.h}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}t^{kn}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right)}_{=\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}h_{j}t^{j}}=\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}t^{kn}\right)\cdot\left(\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}h_{j}t^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}}_{=\sum\limits_{\left(n,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}\underbrace{t^{kn}h_{j}t^{j}}_{=h_{j}t^{kn+j}}=\sum\limits_{\left(n,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{j}t^{kn+j}.

This is an equality between two power series in
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
. If we compare the coefficients of

t^{m}
on both sides of it (where
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
are considered
scalars, not monomials), we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i;\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(n,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{2};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{j}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}.

Now, the definition of
G\left(k,m\right)
yields

\displaystyle G\left(k,m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m;\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i;\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}.

(68)

But the right hand side of this equality can be simplified. Namely, for each

n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}=h_{m-kn}.

(69)

[
Proof of (
69
):
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We must
prove the equality (
69
). If
m-kn<0
, then
h_{m-kn}=0

and
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}=\left(\text{empty
sum}\right)=0
. Thus, if
m-kn<0
, then (
69
) boils down
to
0=0
, which is obviously true. Therefore, for the rest of the proof of
(
69
), we WLOG assume that
m-kn\geq 0
. Hence, the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}
has exactly one addend,
namely the addend for
j=m-kn
. Therefore,
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}=h_{m-kn}
. This proves (
69
).]

Now, (
68
) becomes

\displaystyle G\left(k,m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}\cdot\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
kn+j=m\end{subarray}}h_{j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{m-kn}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.G.frob.16}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)\left(-1\right)^{n}\cdot h_{m-kn}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{m-kn}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{n}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)

(here, we have renamed the summation index
n
as
i
). This proves Theorem

2.20
.

Another proof of Theorem
2.20
is sketched in a footnote in Section

4
below.

3.14
Proofs of the results from
Section
2.8

We shall now prove the results from Section
2.8
. We
begin with Lemma
2.22
. This will rely on the Verschiebung
endomorphisms
\mathbf{v}_{n}
introduced in Definition
2.24
, and on
Proposition
2.25
and the equality (
11
).

Proof 3.36

(Proof of Lemma
2.22
.)
.

Applying (
11
) to
n=k
, we
obtain

\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right)=\begin{cases}kp_{m/k},&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \ .

(70)

Applying Proposition
2.25
to
n=k
,
a=p_{m}
and
b=e_{j}
, we
obtain

\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right),e_{j}\right\rangle.

(71)

Now, we are in one of the following three cases:

Case 1:
We have
m=kj
.

Case 2:
We have
k\nmid m
.

Case 3:
We have neither
m=kj
nor
k\nmid m
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
m=kj
. Thus,
k\mid m

(since
j\in\mathbb{N}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
) and
m/k=j
. Hence,
j=m/k
, so
that the integer
j
is positive (since
m
and
k
are positive). But
(
70
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}kp_{m/k},&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \ =kp_{m/k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }k\mid m\right)

\displaystyle=kp_{j}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }m/k=j\right).

Thus, (
71
) becomes

\displaystyle\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right)}_{=kp_{j}},e_{j}\right\rangle=\left\langle kp_{j},e_{j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle e_{j},kp_{j}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the Hall inner product is symmetric}\right)

\displaystyle=k\underbrace{\left\langle e_{j},p_{j}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.ejpj}, applied to
}n=j\text{)}\end{subarray}}=k\left(-1\right)^{j-1}=\left(-1\right)^{j-1}k.

Comparing this with

\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\underbrace{\left[m=kj\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }m=kj\text{)}\end{subarray}}k=\left(-1\right)^{j-1}k,

we obtain
\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right)\right\rangle=\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\left[m=kj\right]k
. Thus, Lemma

2.22
is proven in Case 1.

A similar (but simpler) argument can be used to prove Lemma

2.22
in Case 2. The main “idea” here is that
k\nmid m
implies
m\neq kj
. The
details are left to the reader.

Let us finally consider Case 3. In this case, we have neither
m=kj
nor

k\nmid m
. In other words, we have
m\neq kj
and
k\mid m
. From
k\mid m
,
we conclude that
m/k
is a positive integer
28
28

28

Indeed, it is positive
since
m
and
k
are positive.
. From
m\neq kj
, we obtain
m/k\neq j
.
Thus, the symmetric functions
p_{m/k}
and
e_{j}
are homogeneous of
different degrees
29
29

29

since
p_{m/k}
is homogeneous of degree
m/k
,
whereas
e_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
j
, and therefore satisfy

\left\langle p_{m/k},e_{j}\right\rangle=0
(by (
2
),
applied to
f=p_{m/k}
and
g=e_{j}
).

Now, (
70
) becomes

\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right)=\begin{cases}kp_{m/k},&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \ =kp_{m/k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }k\mid m\right).

Thus, (
71
) becomes

\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(p_{m}\right)}_{=kp_{m/k}},e_{j}\right\rangle=\left\langle kp_{m/k},e_{j}\right\rangle=k\underbrace{\left\langle p_{m/k},e_{j}\right\rangle}_{=0}=0.

Comparing this with

\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\underbrace{\left[m=kj\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }m\neq kj\text{)}\end{subarray}}k=0,

we obtain
\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{j}\right)\right\rangle=\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\left[m=kj\right]k
. Thus, Lemma

2.22
is proven in Case 3.

We have thus proven Lemma
2.22
in all three Cases 1, 2 and 3.
Thus, Lemma
2.22
always holds.

Next, let us prove a simple property of Hall inner products:

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
m
,
\alpha
and
\beta
be positive integers. Let

a
be a homogeneous symmetric function of degree
\alpha
. Let
b
be a
homogeneous symmetric function of degree
\beta
. Then,
\left\langle p_{m},ab\right\rangle=0
.

Proof 3.38

(Proof of Lemma
3.37
.)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\Lambda_{n}
denote the
n
-th homogeneous component of the graded

\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\Lambda
. Thus,
a\in\Lambda_{\alpha}
and
b\in\Lambda_{\beta}
(since
a
and
b
are homogeneous symmetric functions of
degrees
\alpha
and
\beta
).

But it is known that the family
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}
is a graded basis of the graded
\mathbf{k}
-module

\Lambda
; this means that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, its subfamily
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n}}
is a basis
of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\Lambda_{n}
.
30
30

30

This fact appears,
e.g., in
[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.4.3(j)]
.
Applying this to
n=\alpha
, we
conclude that the subfamily
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\alpha}}
is a basis of
\Lambda_{\alpha}
.
Hence,
a
is a
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of this family
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\alpha}}
(since

a\in\Lambda_{\alpha}
).

We must prove the equality
\left\langle p_{m},ab\right\rangle=0
. Both sides
of this equality depend
\mathbf{k}
-linearly on
a
. Thus, in proving it, we
can WLOG assume that
a
belongs to the family
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\alpha}}
(because we know that
a

is a
\mathbf{k}
-linear combination of this family). In other words, we can
WLOG assume that
a=h_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\alpha}
. Assume this. For similar reasons, we can WLOG assume that

b=h_{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\beta}
. Assume
this, too. Consider these two partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
.

We have
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{\alpha}
and thus
\left|\lambda\right|=\alpha>0
, so that
\lambda\neq\varnothing
. Hence, the
partition
\lambda
has at least one part. Likewise, the partition
\mu
has
at least one part.

Now, let
\lambda\sqcup\mu
be the partition obtained by listing all parts of

\lambda
and of
\mu
and sorting the resulting list in weakly decreasing
order.
31
31

31

For example: If
\lambda=\left(5,3,2\right)
and

\mu=\left(6,4,3,1,1\right)
, then
\lambda\sqcup\mu=\left(6,5,4,3,3,2,1,1\right)
.
Using Definition
3.1
, we can easily see
that
h_{\lambda\sqcup\mu}=h_{\lambda}h_{\mu}
. Comparing this with

\underbrace{a}_{=h_{\lambda}}\underbrace{b}_{=h_{\mu}}=h_{\lambda}h_{\mu}
,
we obtain
ab=h_{\lambda\sqcup\mu}
.

But the partition
\lambda\sqcup\mu
has as many parts as
\lambda
and
\mu

have combined. Thus, the partition
\lambda\sqcup\mu
has at least
2
parts
(since
\lambda
has at least one part, and
\mu
has at least one part).
Therefore,
\lambda\sqcup\mu\neq\left(m\right)
(since the partition

\lambda\sqcup\mu
has at least
2
parts, while the partition
\left(m\right)
has only
1
part). Now, recall that
p_{m}=m_{\left(m\right)}
(where, of course, the two “
m
”s in
“
m_{\left(m\right)}
” mean completely
unrelated things). Thus,

\displaystyle\left\langle\underbrace{p_{m}}_{=m_{\left(m\right)}},\underbrace{ab}_{=h_{\lambda\sqcup\mu}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle m_{\left(m\right)},h_{\lambda\sqcup\mu}\right\rangle=\left\langle h_{\lambda\sqcup\mu},m_{\left(m\right)}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the Hall inner product}\\
\text{is symmetric}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\lambda\sqcup\mu,\left(m\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{eq.hlam-mlam-dual})}\right)

\displaystyle=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\lambda\sqcup\mu\neq\left(m\right)\right).

This proves Lemma
3.37
.

See
[
Grinbe20b
]
for a different proof of Lemma
3.37
, using
the graded dual
\Lambda^{o}
of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
and the
primitivity of the element
p_{m}\in\Lambda
.

We can now prove Proposition
2.23
:

Proof 3.39

(Proof of Proposition
2.23
.)
.

Theorem
2.20
yields

G\left(k,m\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle p_{m},\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\left\langle p_{m},h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle

(72)

(since the Hall inner product is
\mathbf{k}
-bilinear).

Now, we claim that every
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0,m/k\right\}

satisfies

\left\langle p_{m},h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle=0.

(73)

[
Proof of (
73
):
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0,m/k\right\}
. Thus,
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\notin\left\{0,m/k\right\}
. From
i\notin\left\{0,m/k\right\}
, we obtain

i\neq 0
and
i\neq m/k
. From
i\neq m/k
, we obtain
ki\neq m
, so that

m-ki\neq 0
.

We must prove the equality (
73
). If
m-ki<0
,
then
h_{m-ki}=0
, and therefore

\left\langle p_{m},\underbrace{h_{m-ki}}_{=0}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle p_{m},0\right\rangle=0.

Hence, the equality (
73
) is proven if
m-ki<0
.
Thus, for the rest of this proof, we WLOG assume that
m-ki\geq 0
. Combining
this with
m-ki\neq 0
, we obtain
m-ki>0
. Thus,
m-ki
is a positive integer.
Also,
i
is a positive integer (since
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\neq 0
), and
thus
ki
is a positive integer (since
k
is a positive integer).

The map
\mathbf{f}_{k}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
operates by replacing each

x_{i}
by
x_{i}^{k}
in a symmetric function (by the definition of

\mathbf{f}_{k}
). Thus, if
g\in\Lambda
is any homogeneous symmetric
function of some degree
\gamma
, then
\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(g\right)
is a
homogeneous symmetric function of degree
k\gamma
. Applying this to
g=e_{i}

and
\gamma=i
, we conclude that
\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)
is a
homogeneous symmetric function of degree
ki
(since
e_{i}
is a homogeneous
symmetric function of degree
i
). Also,
h_{m-ki}
is a homogeneous symmetric
function of degree
m-ki
.

Hence, Lemma
3.37
(applied to
\alpha=m-ki
,
a=h_{m-ki}
,

\beta=ki
and
b=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)
) yields
\left\langle p_{m},h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle=0
. This
proves (
73
).]

Note that
e_{0}=1
and thus
\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{0}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(1\right)=1
(by the definition of
\mathbf{f}_{k}
).

Note that
m/k>0
(since
m
and
k
are positive). Hence,
m/k\neq 0
. Now, we
are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
k\mid m
.

Case 2:
We have
k\nmid m
.

Let us consider Case 1 first. In this case, we have
k\mid m
. Hence,
m/k
is
a positive integer (since
m
and
k
are positive integers). Thus,
0
and

m/k
are two distinct elements of
\mathbb{N}
(indeed, they are distinct
because
m/k\neq 0
). Lemma
2.22
(applied to
j=m/k
) yields

\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{m/k}\right)\right\rangle=\left(-1\right)^{m/k-1}\underbrace{\left[m=k\left(m/k\right)\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }m=k\left(m/k\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}k=\left(-1\right)^{m/k-1}k.

Now, (
72
) becomes

\displaystyle\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\left\langle p_{m},h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{0}}_{=1}\left\langle p_{m},\underbrace{h_{m-k\cdot 0}}_{=h_{m}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{0}\right)}_{=1}\right\rangle+\left(-1\right)^{m/k}\left\langle p_{m},\underbrace{h_{m-k\cdot m/k}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{0}\\
\text{(since }m-k\cdot m/k=0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{m/k}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0,m/k\right\}}\left(-1\right)^{i}\underbrace{\left\langle p_{m},h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.hall-pmGkm.zeroes}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have split off the addends for }i=0\text{ and for }i=m/k\\
\text{from the sum (since }0\text{ and }m/k\text{ are two distinct elements of
}\mathbb{N}\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\langle p_{m},h_{m}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left\langle h_{m},p_{m}\right\rangle\\
\text{(since the Hall
inner}\\
\text{product is symmetric)}\end{subarray}}+\left(-1\right)^{m/k}\left\langle p_{m},\underbrace{h_{0}}_{=1}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{m/k}\right)\right\rangle+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0,m/k\right\}}\left(-1\right)^{i}0}_{=0}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\langle h_{m},p_{m}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.hjpj},}\\
\text{applied to
}n=m\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\left(-1\right)^{m/k}\underbrace{\left\langle p_{m},\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{m/k}\right)\right\rangle}_{=\left(-1\right)^{m/k-1}k}=1+\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{m/k}\left(-1\right)^{m/k-1}}_{=-1}k=1-k.

Comparing this with

1-\underbrace{\left[k\mid m\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }k\mid m\text{)}\end{subarray}}k=1-k,

we obtain
\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle=1-\left[k\mid m\right]k
. Hence, Proposition
2.23
is proven in Case 1.

Case 2 is similar to Case 1, but simpler because we no longer need to split
off the addend for
i=m/k
from the sum (since
m/k\notin\mathbb{N}
in this
case). We leave it to the reader.

We have now proven Proposition
2.23
both in Case 1 and in
Case 2. Hence, Proposition
2.23
always holds.

Theorem
2.21
will follow from Proposition

2.23
using the following general criterion for generating
sets of
\Lambda
:

Proposition 3.40
.

For each positive integer
m
, let
v_{m}\in\Lambda

be a homogeneous symmetric function of degree
m
.

Assume that
\left\langle p_{m},v_{m}\right\rangle
is an invertible element
of
\mathbf{k}
for each positive integer
m
.

Then, the family
\left(v_{m}\right)_{m\geq 1}=\left(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\ldots\right)
is an algebraically independent generating set of the
commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\Lambda
.

Proof 3.41

(Proof of Proposition
3.40
.)
.

Proposition

3.40
is
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.5.24]
.

Proof 3.42

(Proof of Theorem
2.21
.)
.

Let
m
be a positive integer.
Proposition
2.23
yields that

\displaystyle\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=1-\underbrace{\left[k\mid m\right]}_{=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}}k=1-\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \cdot k

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1-1\cdot k,&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
1-0\cdot k,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \ =\begin{cases}1-k,&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
1,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}\ \ \ \ .

Hence,
\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle
is an
invertible element of
\mathbf{k}
(because both
1-k
and
1
are invertible
elements of
\mathbf{k}
).

Forget that we fixed
m
. We thus have showed that
\left\langle p_{m},G\left(k,m\right)\right\rangle
is an invertible element of

\mathbf{k}
for each positive integer
m
. Also, clearly, for each positive
integer
m
, the element
G\left(k,m\right)\in\Lambda
is a homogeneous
symmetric function of degree
m
. Thus, Proposition
3.40

(applied to
v_{m}=G\left(k,m\right)
) shows that the family
\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)_{m\geq 1}=\left(G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots\right)
is an algebraically
independent generating set of the commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\Lambda
.
This proves Theorem
2.21
.

3.15
Proof of Theorem
2.27

Proof 3.43

(Proof of Theorem
2.27
.)
.

The
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra
\Lambda

is both commutative and cocommutative (by
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.3.7(a)]
).

The antipode
S
of this Hopf algebra
\Lambda
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra
homomorphism (by
[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.4.3(g)]
).

(a)
The map
\mathbf{f}_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra
homomorphism (by
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.9.9(d)]
, applied to
n=k
). The map

\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism (by

[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.9.10(e)]
, applied to
n=k
). Thus, we have shown that
all three maps
\mathbf{f}_{k}
,
S
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}
are
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphisms. Hence, their composition
\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}

\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism as well. In
other words,
U_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism (since

U_{k}=\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}
). This proves Theorem

2.27

(a)
.

(b)
Recall (from
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 1.5.11(a)]
) the following fact:

Claim 1:
If
H
is a
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra and
A
is a commutative

\mathbf{k}
-algebra, then the convolution
f\star g
of any two
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms
f,g:H\rightarrow A
is again a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism.

The following fact is dual to Claim 1:

Claim 2:
If
H
is a
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra and
C
is a
cocommutative
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra, then the convolution
f\star g
of any
two
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra homomorphisms
f,g:C\rightarrow H
is again a

\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra homomorphism.

(See
[
GriRei20
, solution to Exercise 1.5.11(h)]
for why exactly Claim 2 is
dual to Claim 1, and how it can be proved.)

Theorem
2.27

(a)
yields that the map
U_{k}
is a

\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism. Hence,
U_{k}
is both a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism and a
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra homomorphism.

Now, recall that
\Lambda
is commutative, and that
\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}
and
U_{k}
are two
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms
from
\Lambda
to
\Lambda
. Hence, Claim 1 (applied to

,

A=\Lambda
,
f=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}
and

) shows

\mathbf{k}

V_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism (since
V_{k}=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\star U_{k}
).

Next, recall that
\Lambda
is cocommutative, and that
\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}
and
U_{k}
are two
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra homomorphisms
from
\Lambda
to
\Lambda
. Hence, Claim 2 (applied to
H=\Lambda
,

C=\Lambda
,
f=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}

) shows

\mathbf{k}

V_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra homomorphism (since

So we know that the map
V_{k}

\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
and a
\mathbf{k}

V_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra homomorphism, thus a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra
homomorphism
32
32

32

since any
\mathbf{k}
-bialgebra homomorphism between
two
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebras is automatically a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra
homomorphism
. This proves Theorem
2.27

(b)
.

(c)
The map
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism;
thus,
\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(1\right)=1
. Now, we have

\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(h_{m}\right)=\begin{cases}h_{m/k},&\text{if }k\mid m;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid m\end{cases}

(74)

for each

. (Indeed, if

\mathbf{v}_{k}
. But if

, since
h_{0}=1
.)

We have

S\left(h_{n}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{n}e_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(75)

(This follows from
[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.4.1(iii)]
.)

Each
i\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(h_{ki}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}h_{ki/k},&\text{if }k\mid ki;\\
0,&\text{if }k\nmid ki\end{cases}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.vh}), applied to
}m=ki\right)

\displaystyle=h_{ki/k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }k\mid ki\right)

\displaystyle=h_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }ki/k=i\right)

(76)

and

\displaystyle U_{k}\left(h_{ki}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}\right)\left(h_{ki}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }U_{k}=\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(S\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(h_{ki}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{i}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.vkhj1}))}\end{subarray}}\right)\right)=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(\underbrace{S\left(h_{i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{i}e_{i}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.Sh}))}\end{subarray}}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(\left(-1\right)^{i}e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-1\right)^{i}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)

(77)

(since the map
\mathbf{f}_{k}

\mathbf{k}
-linear).

On the other hand, if

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}h_{j/k},&\text{if }k\mid j;\\

\displaystyle=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }k\nmid j\right)

(78)

and

\displaystyle U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}\right)\left(h_{j}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }U_{k}=\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\circ\mathbf{v}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S\right)\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by

\displaystyle=0

(79)

(since the map
\mathbf{f}_{k}\circ S
is
\mathbf{k}
-linear).

Let
\Delta_{\Lambda}
be the comultiplication
\Delta:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
of the
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra
\Lambda
. Let

m_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\otimes\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
be the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map sending each pure tensor

to

. Definition
2.26
then yields

\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\star U_{k}=m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta_{\Lambda}
. Thus,

\displaystyle V_{k}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\star U_{k}=m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\underbrace{\Delta_{\Lambda}}_{=\Delta}

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta.

(80)

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
(not to be mistaken for the map
m_{\Lambda}
). Then,

[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.3.6(iii)]
(applied to
n=m

\displaystyle\Delta\left(h_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i+j=m}h_{i}\otimes h_{j}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{where the sum ranges over all pairs
}\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\text{ with }i+j=m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}\otimes h_{j}

(here, we have substituted
\left(m-j,j\right)
for
\left(i,j\right)

in the sum). Applying the map
\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}
to both sides of this equality, we obtain

\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(\Delta\left(h_{m}\right)\right)=\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}\otimes h_{j}\right)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}\otimes U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right).

Applying the map
m_{\Lambda}
to both sides of this equality, we find

\displaystyle m_{\Lambda}\left(\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(\Delta\left(h_{m}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}\otimes U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }m_{\Lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}}_{=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)-\sum\limits_{j=m+1}^{\infty}\underbrace{h_{m-j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }m-j<0\\
\text{(because }j\geq m+1>m\text{))}\end{subarray}}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j=m+1}^{\infty}0U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)}_{=0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\mid j\end{subarray}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\nmid j\end{subarray}}h_{m-j}\underbrace{U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.Ukhj2}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since each }j\in\mathbb{N}\text{
satisfies either }k\mid j\text{ or }k\nmid j\text{ (but not both)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\mid j\end{subarray}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\nmid j\end{subarray}}h_{m-j}0}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\mid j\end{subarray}}h_{m-j}U_{k}\left(h_{j}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}h_{m-ki}\underbrace{U_{k}\left(h_{ki}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{i}\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.Ukhj1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have substituted }ki\text{ for
}j\text{ in the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right).

Comparing this with

G\left(k,m\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Theorem \ref{thm.G.frob}}\right),

we obtain

G\left(k,m\right)=m_{\Lambda}\left(\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(\Delta\left(h_{m}\right)\right)\right)=\underbrace{\left(m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=V_{k}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.Uk.main.c.conv-def}))}\end{subarray}}\left(h_{m}\right)=V_{k}\left(h_{m}\right).

This proves Theorem
2.27

(c)
.

(d)
Let us recall a few facts from
[
GriRei20
]
.

From

n
and

satisfy

\mathbf{v}_{n}\left(p_{m}\right)=\begin{cases}np_{m/n},&\text{if }n\mid m;\\

.

Proof of Claim 1:
We are in one of the following two cases:

.

We have

Applying the map

\displaystyle=k\left(-p_{n/k}\right)=-kp_{n/k}.
