Theorem
2.27

(a)
shows that the map
U_{k}
is a

\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism. Hence,
U_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism. Thus,
U_{k}\left(1\right)=1
.

Now, let
\Delta_{\Lambda}
be the comultiplication
\Delta:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
of the
\mathbf{k}
-coalgebra
\Lambda
. Let

m_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\otimes\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
be the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map sending each pure tensor
a\otimes b\in\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
to

ab\in\Lambda
. Then, the definition of
V_{k}
yields
V_{k}=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\star U_{k}=m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta_{\Lambda}
(by the definition of convolution).

But
[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.3.6(i)]
yields
\Delta_{\Lambda}\left(p_{n}\right)=1\otimes p_{n}+p_{n}\otimes 1
. Now,

\displaystyle\underbrace{V_{k}}_{=m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta_{\Lambda}}\left(p_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(m_{\Lambda}\circ\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\circ\Delta_{\Lambda}\right)\left(p_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}\left(\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(\underbrace{\Delta_{\Lambda}\left(p_{n}\right)}_{=1\otimes p_{n}+p_{n}\otimes 1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}\left(\underbrace{\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)\left(1\otimes p_{n}+p_{n}\otimes 1\right)}_{=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(1\right)\otimes U_{k}\left(p_{n}\right)+\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(p_{n}\right)\otimes U_{k}\left(1\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}\left(\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(1\right)\otimes U_{k}\left(p_{n}\right)+\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(p_{n}\right)\otimes U_{k}\left(1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(1\right)}_{=1}\cdot\underbrace{U_{k}\left(p_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=-\left[k\mid n\right]kp_{n}\\
\text{(by Claim 1)}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\left(p_{n}\right)}_{=p_{n}}\cdot\underbrace{U_{k}\left(1\right)}_{=1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}m_{\Lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left[k\mid n\right]kp_{n}+p_{n}=\left(1-\left[k\mid n\right]k\right)p_{n}.

This proves Theorem
2.27

(d)
.

3.16
Proof of Corollary
2.28

Proof 3.44

(Proof of Corollary
2.28
.)
.

Recall that the family
\left(h_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}=\left(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots\right)
generates

\Lambda
as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra. Hence, each
g\in\Lambda
can be written
as a polynomial in
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots
. Applying this to
g=p_{n}
, we
conclude that
p_{n}
can be written as a polynomial in
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots
. In other words, there exists a polynomial
f\in\mathbf{k}\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]
such that

p_{n}=f\left(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots\right).

(84)

Consider this
f
. We shall show that this
f
satisfies
(
12
). This will clearly prove Corollary
2.28
.

Consider the map
V_{k}
defined in Theorem
2.27
. Theorem

2.27

(c)
yields that
V_{k}\left(h_{m}\right)=G\left(k,m\right)
for each positive integer
m
. In other words,

\left(V_{k}\left(h_{1}\right),V_{k}\left(h_{2}\right),V_{k}\left(h_{3}\right),\ldots\right)=\left(G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots\right).

(85)

The map
V_{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism (by Theorem

2.27

(b)
), and thus is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism. Hence, it commutes with polynomials over
\mathbf{k}
. Thus,

\displaystyle V_{k}\left(f\left(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots\right)\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(V_{k}\left(h_{1}\right),V_{k}\left(h_{2}\right),V_{k}\left(h_{3}\right),\ldots\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.cor.p-via-G.1})}\right).

Now, applying the map
V_{k}
to both sides of the equality
(
84
), we obtain

V_{k}\left(p_{n}\right)=V_{k}\left(f\left(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots\right)\right)=f\left(G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots\right).

Comparing this with

V_{k}\left(p_{n}\right)=\left(1-\left[k\mid n\right]k\right)p_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Theorem \ref{thm.Uk.main}
{(d)}}\right),

we obtain

\left(1-\left[k\mid n\right]k\right)p_{n}=f\left(G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots\right).

Thus, we have shown that our
f
satisfies (
12
). As we
said, this proves Corollary
2.28
.

4
Proof of the Liu–Polo conjecture

Let us recall a well-known partial order on the set of partitions of a given

n\in\mathbb{N}
:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We define a binary relation

\triangleright
on the set
\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n}
as follows: Two
partitions
\lambda,\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n}
shall satisfy

\lambda\triangleright\mu
if and only if we have

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}\geq\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }k\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}.

This relation
\triangleright
is the greater-or-equal relation of a partial
order on
\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n}
, which is known as the

dominance order
(or the
majorization order
).

This definition is precisely
[
GriRei20
, Definition 2.2.7]
. Note that if we
replace “for each
k\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
” by “for each
k\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
” in this definition, then the
relation
\triangleright
does not change.

Our goal in this section is to prove the conjecture made in
[
LiuPol19
, Remark
1.4.5]
. We state this conjecture as follows:
33
33

33

Note that

\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)
is a partition whenever
n>1
is an integer.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
n
be an integer such that
n>1
. Then:

(a)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n};\\
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}.

(b)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}.

Example 4.3
.

For this example, let
n=3
. Then,
n-1=2
and
2n-1=5
. Hence, the left hand
side of the equality in Theorem
4.2

(b)
is

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{5};\\
\left(2,2,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=m_{\left(2,2,1\right)}+m_{\left(2,1,1,1\right)}+m_{\left(1,1,1,1,1\right)}.

Meanwhile, the right hand side of the equality in Theorem
4.2

(b)
is

\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{1}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(2,2-i,1^{i+1}\right)}=s_{\left(2,2,1\right)}-s_{\left(2,1,1,1\right)}.

Thus, Theorem
4.2

(b)
claims that
m_{\left(2,2,1\right)}+m_{\left(2,1,1,1\right)}+m_{\left(1,1,1,1,1\right)}=s_{\left(2,2,1\right)}-s_{\left(2,1,1,1\right)}
in this case.

We will pave our way to the proof of Theorem
4.2
by several
lemmas. We begin with a particularly simple one:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
n
be an integer such that
n>1
. Let
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1}
. Then,
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda
if and only if all positive integers
i
satisfy

\lambda_{i}<n
.

Proof 4.5
.

This simple proof (an exercise in following Definition
4.1
) is
left to the reader.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
n
be an integer such that
n>1
. Let
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n}
. Then,
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda
if and only if all positive integers
i
satisfy

\lambda_{i}<n
.

Proof 4.7

(Proof of Lemma
4.6
.)
.

This is analogous to the proof of Lemma

4.4
.

The next lemma identifies the left hand side of Theorem
4.2

(a)
as the Petrie symmetric function
G\left(n,n\right)
, and the
left hand side of Theorem
4.2

(b)
as the Petrie symmetric
function
G\left(n,2n-1\right)
:

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
n
be an integer such that
n>1
. Then:

(a)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n};\\
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=G\left(n,n\right).

(b)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=G\left(n,2n-1\right).

Proof 4.9
.

(b)
Proposition
2.3

(c)
(applied to
k=n

and
m=2n-1
) yields

G\left(n,2n-1\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=2n-1;\\
\alpha_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=2n-1;\\
\lambda_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}.

(86)

But Lemma
4.4
yields the following equality of summation
signs:

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\lambda_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=2n-1;\\
\lambda_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}.

Hence,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=2n-1;\\
\lambda_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}.

Comparing this with (
86
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=G\left(n,2n-1\right).

This proves Corollary
4.8

(b)
.

(a)
This is analogous to Corollary
4.8

(b)
, but
uses Lemma
4.6
instead of Lemma
4.4
.

It was Corollary
4.8
that led the author to introduce and study the
Petrie symmetric functions
G\left(k,m\right)
in general, even if little
of their general properties has proven relevant to Theorem
4.2
.

The next proposition gives a simple formula for certain kinds of Petrie
symmetric functions:

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
. Then,

G\left(n,n+k\right)=h_{n+k}-h_{k}p_{n}.

Proposition
4.10
can be viewed as a particular case of Theorem

2.20
(applied to
n
and
n+k
instead of
k
and
m
), after
realizing that in the sum on the right hand side of Theorem
2.20
,
only the first two addends will (potentially) be nonzero in this case.
However, let us give an independent proof of the proposition.

Proof 4.11

(Proof of Proposition
4.10
.)
.

From
k\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, we obtain
k<n
and thus
n+k<n+n
. Thus we conclude:

Observation 1:
A monomial of degree
n+k
cannot have more than one
variable appear in it with exponent
\geq n
(since this would require it to
have degree
\geq n+n>n+k
).

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{k}
be the set of all monomials of degree
k
. The
definition of
h_{k}
shows that
h_{k}
is the sum of all monomials of degree

k
. In other words,

h_{k}=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}}\mathfrak{m}.

(87)

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}
be the set of all monomials of degree
n+k
. The
definition of
h_{n+k}
shows that
h_{n+k}
is the sum of all monomials of
degree
n+k
. In other words,

h_{n+k}=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}}\mathfrak{n}.

(88)

Let
\mathfrak{N}
be the set of all monomials of degree
n+k
in which all
exponents are
<n
. These monomials are exactly the
\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}
for

\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}
satisfying
\left|\alpha\right|=n+k

and
\left(\alpha_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\right)
. Hence,

\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n+k;\\
\alpha_{i}<n\text{
for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}.

(89)

But Proposition
2.3

(c)
(applied to
n
and
n+k

instead of
k
and
m
) yields

G\left(n,n+k\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n+k;\\
\alpha_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n+k;\\
\lambda_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}.

Hence,

G\left(n,n+k\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n+k;\\
\alpha_{i}<n\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}

(90)

(by (
89
)).

Clearly, the set
\mathfrak{N}
is a subset of
\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}
, and
furthermore its complement
\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N}
is the
set of all monomials of degree
n+k
in which at least one exponent is
\geq n
. Hence, the map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{k}\times\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N},

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{m},i\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathfrak{m}\cdot x_{i}^{n}

is well-defined (because if
\mathfrak{m}
is a monomial of degree
k
, and if

i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
, then
\mathfrak{m}\cdot x_{i}^{n}
is a
monomial of degree
k+n=n+k
, and the variable
x_{i}
appears in it with
exponent
\geq n
). This map is furthermore surjective (for simple reasons)
and injective (in fact, if
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N}
, then
\mathfrak{n}
is a monomial of degree
n+k
, and thus
Observation 1 yields that there is
at most
one variable
x_{i}
that
appears in
\mathfrak{n}
with exponent
\geq n
; but this means that the only
preimage of
\mathfrak{n}
under our map is
\left(\dfrac{\mathfrak{n}}{x_{i}^{n}},i\right)
). Hence, this map is a bijection. We can thus use it
to substitute
\mathfrak{m}\cdot x_{i}^{n}
for
\mathfrak{n}
in the sum

\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}
.
We thus obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(\mathfrak{m},i\right)\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}\times\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}}\mathfrak{m}\cdot x_{i}^{n}=\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathfrak{M}_{k}}\mathfrak{m}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{k}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.h2n-1.hk=}))}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}}x_{i}^{n}}_{=p_{n}}

\displaystyle=h_{k}p_{n}.

(91)

But (
88
) becomes

\displaystyle h_{n+k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}}\mathfrak{n}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=G\left(n,n+k\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.h2n-1.su1pet}))}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\setminus\mathfrak{N}}\mathfrak{n}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{k}p_{n}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.h2n-1.su2}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}\mathfrak{N}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}_{n+k}\right)

\displaystyle=G\left(n,n+k\right)+h_{k}p_{n}.

In other words,

G\left(n,n+k\right)=h_{n+k}-h_{k}p_{n}.

This proves Proposition
4.10
.

We note in passing that the idea used in the above proof of Proposition

4.10
can be generalized to yield a second proof of Theorem

2.20
, using an inclusion/exclusion argument.
34
34

34

Here is an
outline of this second proof: For any positive integer
k
and any

m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle G\left(k,m\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m;\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m;\\
\alpha_{i}\geq k\text{ for all }i\in I\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\prod\limits_{i\in I}x_{i}^{k}\right)\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\beta\right|=m-k\left|I\right|\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by an infinite-set version of the
inclusion-exclusion principle}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{I\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}}}_{=\sum\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\};\\
\left|I\right|=p\end{subarray}}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{i\in I}x_{i}^{k}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\beta\right|=m-k\left|I\right|\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}}_{=h_{m-k\left|I\right|}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\};\\
\left|I\right|=p\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}}_{=\left(-1\right)^{p}}\left(\prod\limits_{i\in I}x_{i}^{k}\right)\cdot\underbrace{h_{m-k\left|I\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{m-kp}\\
\text{(since }\left|I\right|=p\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{p}h_{m-kp}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\};\\
\left|I\right|=p\end{subarray}}\ \ \prod\limits_{i\in I}x_{i}^{k}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{p}\right)\\
\text{(this is easy to check)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{p}h_{m-kp}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m-ki}\cdot\mathbf{f}_{k}\left(e_{i}\right).

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
n
be an integer such that
n>1
. Then:

(a)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n};\\
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=h_{n}-p_{n}.

(b)
We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=h_{2n-1}-h_{n-1}p_{n}.

Proof 4.13
.

(b)
Corollary
4.8

(b)
yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=G\left(n,2n-1\right)=G\left(n,n+\left(n-1\right)\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }2n-1=n+\left(n-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=h_{n+\left(n-1\right)}-h_{n-1}p_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop.h2n-1}, applied to }k=n-1\right)

\displaystyle=h_{2n-1}-h_{n-1}p_{n}.

This proves Corollary
4.12

(b)
.

(a)
Corollary
4.8

(a)
yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n};\\
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=G\left(n,n\right)=G\left(n,n+0\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{h_{n+0}}_{=h_{n}}-\underbrace{h_{0}}_{=1}p_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop.h2n-1}, applied to
}k=0\right)

\displaystyle=h_{n}-p_{n}.

This proves Corollary
4.12

(a)
.

Our next claim is an easy consequence of Proposition
1.1
:

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then,

h_{n}-p_{n}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}.

Proof 4.15
.

Proposition
1.1
yields

\displaystyle p_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)}=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{0}}_{=1}\underbrace{s_{\left(n-0,1^{0}\right)}}_{=s_{\left(n-0\right)}=s_{\left(n\right)}=h_{n}}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)}

\displaystyle=h_{n}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)},

so that

\displaystyle h_{n}-p_{n}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\underbrace{\left(-\left(-1\right)^{i}\right)}_{=\left(-1\right)^{i-1}}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{i-1}s_{\left(n-i,1^{i}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}

(here, we have substituted
i+1
for
i
in the sum).

We can now immediately prove Theorem
4.2

(a)
:

Proof 4.16

(Proof of Theorem
4.2

(a)
.)
.

Corollary
4.12

(a)
yields

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{n};\\
\left(n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=h_{n}-p_{n}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Corollary \ref{cor.p-as-sum-2}}\right).

This proves Theorem
4.2

(a)
.

We shall use the
skewing operators

f^{\perp}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
for all
f\in\Lambda
as defined in
[
GriRei20
, §2.8]
or in

[
Macdon95
, Chapter I, Section 5, Example 3]
. The easiest way to define
them (following
[
Macdon95
, Chapter I, Section 5, Example 3]
) is as
follows: For each
f\in\Lambda
, we let
f^{\perp}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda

be the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map adjoint to the map
L_{f}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda,\ g\mapsto fg
(that is, to the map that multiplies every
element of
\Lambda
by
f
) with respect to the Hall inner product. That is,

f^{\perp}
is the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map from
\Lambda
to
\Lambda
that
satisfies

\left\langle g,f^{\perp}\left(a\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle fg,a\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }a\in\Lambda\text{ and
}g\in\Lambda.

It is not hard to show that such an operator
f^{\perp}
exists
35
35

35

This
is not completely automatic: Not every
\mathbf{k}
-linear map from
\Lambda

to
\Lambda
has an adjoint with respect to the Hall inner product! (For
example, the
\mathbf{k}
-linear map
\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
that sends
each Schur function
s_{\lambda}
to
1
has none.) The reason why the map

L_{f}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda,\ g\mapsto fg
has an adjoint is that when

f
is homogeneous of degree
k
, this map
L_{f}
sends each graded component

\Lambda_{m}
of
\Lambda
to
\Lambda_{m+k}
, and both of these graded
components
\Lambda_{m}
and
\Lambda_{m+k}
are
\mathbf{k}
-modules with

finite
bases. (The case when
f
is not homogeneous can be reduced to
the case when
f
is homogeneous, since each
f\in\Lambda
is a sum of
finitely many homogeneous elements.)
. The definition of
f^{\perp}
in

[
GriRei20
, §2.8]
is different but equivalent (because of

[
GriRei20
, Proposition 2.8.2(i)]
). One of the most important properties of
skewing operators is the following fact (
[
GriRei20
, (2.8.2)]
):

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\lambda
and
\mu
be any two partitions. Then,

s_{\mu}^{\perp}\left(s_{\lambda}\right)=s_{\lambda/\mu}.

(92)

(Here,
s_{\lambda/\mu}
is a skew Schur function, defined in Subsection

3.3
.)

Using skewing operators, we can define another helpful family of operators on

\Lambda
:

Definition 4.18
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define a map
\mathbf{B}_{m}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
by setting

\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(f\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}e_{i}^{\perp}f\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }f\in\Lambda\text{.}

It is known (
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.9.1(a)]
) that this map
\mathbf{B}_{m}

is well-defined and
\mathbf{k}
-linear.

(Actually, the well-definedness of
\mathbf{B}_{m}
is easy to check: If

f\in\Lambda
has degree
d
, then all integers
i>d
satisfy
e_{i}^{\perp}f=0
for degree reasons, and thus the sum
\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}e_{i}^{\perp}f
has only finitely many nonzero addends.
The
\mathbf{k}
-linearity of
\mathbf{B}_{m}
is even clearer.)

The operators
\mathbf{B}_{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
have first appeared in
Zelevinsky’s
[
Zelevi81
, §4.20]
(in the different-looking but secretly
equivalent setting of a PSH-algebra), where they are credited to J. N.
Bernstein. They have since been dubbed the
Bernstein creation operators

and proved useful in various contexts (e.g., the definition of the
“dual immaculate functions” in

[
BBSSZ13
]
takes them for inspiration). One of their most fundamental
properties is the following fact (which originates in
[
Zelevi81
, 4.20, (
\ast\ast
)]
and appears implicitly in
[
Macdon95
, Chapter I, Section 5, Example
29]
):

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
\lambda
be any partition. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfy
m\geq\lambda_{1}
. Then,

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}e_{i}^{\perp}s_{\lambda}=s_{\left(m,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots\right)}.

(93)

See
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.9.1(b)]
for a proof of Proposition

4.19
. Thus, if
\lambda
is any partition, and if
m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
m\geq\lambda_{1}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(s_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}e_{i}^{\perp}s_{\lambda}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }\mathbf{B}_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=s_{\left(m,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\ldots\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{eq.schur-row-adder-1})}\right).

(94)

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(h_{n}\right)=h_{m}h_{n}-h_{m+1}h_{n-1}
.

Proof 4.21

(Proof of Lemma
4.20
.)
.

We have
e_{0}=1
and thus
e_{0}^{\perp}=1^{\perp}=\operatorname*{id}
. Hence,
e_{0}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)=h_{n}
.

We shall use the notion of skew Schur functions
s_{\lambda/\mu}
(as in
Subsection
3.3
). Recall that
s_{\lambda/\mu}=0
when

\mu\not\subseteq\lambda
.

From
e_{1}=s_{\left(1\right)}
and
h_{n}=s_{\left(n\right)}
, we
obtain

e_{1}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)=s_{\left(1\right)}^{\perp}\left(s_{\left(n\right)}\right)=s_{\left(n\right)/\left(1\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.Bmhn.skew})}\right).

But it is easy to see that
s_{\left(n\right)/\left(1\right)}=s_{\left(n-1\right)}
. (Indeed, this follows from the combinatorial
definition of skew Schur functions, since the skew Ferrers diagram of
\left(n\right)/\left(1\right)
can be obtained from the Ferrers diagram of

\left(n-1\right)
by parallel shift
36
36

36

See
[
GriRei20
, §2.3]

for the notions we are using here.
. Alternatively, this follows easily from
Theorem
3.7
, because
s_{\left(n-1\right)}=h_{n-1}
.)

Thus, we obtain

e_{1}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)=s_{\left(n\right)/\left(1\right)}=s_{\left(n-1\right)}=h_{n-1}.

For each integer
i>1
, we have

\displaystyle e_{i}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=s_{\left(1^{i}\right)}^{\perp}\left(s_{\left(n\right)}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }e_{i}=s_{\left(1^{i}\right)}\text{ and }h_{n}=s_{\left(n\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=s_{\left(n\right)/\left(1^{i}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.Bmhn.skew})}\right)

\displaystyle=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\left(1^{i}\right)\not\subseteq\left(n\right)\text{ (because }i>1\text{)}\right).

(95)

Now, the definition of
\mathbf{B}_{m}
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(h_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}e_{i}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{0}}_{=1}\underbrace{h_{m+0}}_{=h_{m}}\underbrace{e_{0}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{=h_{n}}+\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{1}}_{=-1}h_{m+1}\underbrace{e_{1}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{=h_{n-1}}+\sum\limits_{i\geq 2}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}\underbrace{e_{i}^{\perp}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.Bmhn.3}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=h_{m}h_{n}-h_{m+1}h_{n-1}.

Corollary 4.22
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then,
\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}\right)=0
.

Proof 4.23
.

Apply Lemma
4.20
to
m=n-1
and simplify.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
n
be a positive integer. Then,

\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(p_{n}\right)=h_{m}p_{n}-h_{m+n}
.

Proof 4.25
.

This is
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.9.1(f)]
. But here is a more direct proof: We
will use the comultiplication
\Delta:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes\Lambda

of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
(see
[
GriRei20
, §2.3]
). Here and in the
following, the “
\otimes
” sign denotes

\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}
. The power-sum symmetric function
p_{n}
is
primitive
37
37

37

Recall that an element
x
of a Hopf algebra
H
is said to
be
primitive
if the comultiplication
\Delta_{H}
of
H
satisfies

\Delta_{H}\left(x\right)=1\otimes x+x\otimes 1
.
(see
[
GriRei20
, Proposition
2.3.6(i)]
); thus,

\Delta\left(p_{n}\right)=1\otimes p_{n}+p_{n}\otimes 1.

Hence, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the definition of
e_{i}^{\perp}
given in

[
GriRei20
, Definition 2.8.1]
(not the equivalent definition we gave above)
yields

e_{i}^{\perp}\left(p_{n}\right)=\left\langle e_{i},1\right\rangle p_{n}+\left\langle e_{i},p_{n}\right\rangle 1.

(96)

Now, the definition of
\mathbf{B}_{m}
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(p_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}\underbrace{e_{i}^{\perp}\left(p_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left\langle e_{i},1\right\rangle p_{n}+\left\langle e_{i},p_{n}\right\rangle 1\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.Bmpn.eipn}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}\left(\left\langle e_{i},1\right\rangle p_{n}+\left\langle e_{i},p_{n}\right\rangle 1\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}\cdot\left\langle e_{i},1\right\rangle p_{n}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{0}h_{m+0}\cdot\left\langle e_{0},1\right\rangle p_{n}\\
\text{(because the
Hall inner product }\left\langle e_{i},1\right\rangle\\
\text{equals }0\text{
whenever }i\neq 0\text{ (by (\ref{eq.Hall.graded})),}\\
\text{and thus the only
nonzero addend of this}\\
\text{sum is the addend for }i=0\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left(-1\right)^{i}h_{m+i}\cdot\left\langle e_{i},p_{n}\right\rangle 1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{m+n}\cdot\left\langle e_{n},p_{n}\right\rangle 1\\
\text{(because the
Hall inner product }\left\langle e_{i},p_{n}\right\rangle\\
\text{equals
}0\text{ whenever }i\neq n\text{ (by (\ref{eq.Hall.graded})),}\\
\text{and thus
the only nonzero addend of this}\\
\text{sum is the addend for }i=n\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{0}}_{=1}\underbrace{h_{m+0}}_{=h_{m}}\cdot\underbrace{\left\langle e_{0},1\right\rangle}_{=\left\langle 1,1\right\rangle=1}p_{n}+\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{m+n}\cdot\underbrace{\left\langle e_{n},p_{n}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{n-1}\\
\text{(by Proposition \ref{prop.ejpj})}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=h_{m}p_{n}+\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{n}h_{m+n}\cdot\left(-1\right)^{n-1}1}_{=-h_{m+n}}=h_{m}p_{n}-h_{m+n}.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then,

\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}-p_{n}\right)=h_{2n-1}-h_{n-1}p_{n}.

Proof 4.27
.

The map
\mathbf{B}_{n-1}
is
\mathbf{k}
-linear. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}-p_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by Corollary
\ref{cor.Bn-1Hn})}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(p_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{n-1}p_{n}-h_{\left(n-1\right)+n}\\
\text{(by Lemma
\ref{lem.Bmpn},}\\
\text{applied to }m=n-1\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=-\left(h_{n-1}p_{n}-h_{\left(n-1\right)+n}\right)=\underbrace{h_{\left(n-1\right)+n}}_{=h_{2n-1}}-h_{n-1}p_{n}=h_{2n-1}-h_{n-1}p_{n}.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Then,

\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}-p_{n}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}.

Proof 4.29

(Proof of Lemma
4.28
.)
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}-p_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Corollary \ref{cor.p-as-sum-2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(s_{\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.schur-row-adder-2}), applied
to }m=n-1\\
\text{and }\lambda=\left(n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)\\
\text{(since
}n-1\geq n-1-i\text{))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\text{ is }\mathbf{k}\text{-linear}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}.

Now the proof of Theorem
4.2

(b)
is a trivial
concatenation of equalities:

Proof 4.30

(Proof of Theorem
4.2

(b)
.)
.

Corollary
4.12

(b)
yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{2n-1};\\
\left(n-1,n-1,1\right)\triangleright\lambda\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=h_{2n-1}-h_{n-1}p_{n}=\mathbf{B}_{n-1}\left(h_{n}-p_{n}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.Bm1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\left(-1\right)^{i}s_{\left(n-1,n-1-i,1^{i+1}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.Bm2}}\right).

5
Final remarks

5.1
SageMath code

The SageMath computer algebra system
[
SageMath
]
does not (yet) natively
know the Petrie symmetric functions
G\left(k,m\right)
; but they can be
easily constructed in it. For example, the code that follows computes

G\left(k,n\right)
expanded in the Schur basis:

⬇

,

)

[

]

Partitions
(

,

-

)))

5.2
Understanding the Petrie numbers

Combining Corollary
2.9
with Theorem
2.14

yields an explicit expression of all coefficients in the expansion of a Petrie
symmetric function
G\left(k,m\right)
in the Schur basis. It would stand
to reason if the identity in Theorem
4.2

(b)
(whose left
hand side is
G\left(n,2n-1\right)
) could be obtained from this
expression. Surprisingly, we have been unable to do so, which suggests that
the description of
\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)
in Theorem
2.14
might not be optimal.

As to
\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{k}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)
, we do
not have an explicit description at all, unless we count the recursive one
that can be extracted from the proof in
[
GorWil74
]
.

5.3
MNable symmetric functions

Combining Theorem
2.16
with Proposition
2.7
,
we conclude that for any
k>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the symmetric function

G\left(k,m\right)\in\Lambda
has the following property: For any
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}
, its product
G\left(k,m\right)\cdot s_{\mu}
with

s_{\mu}
can be written in the form
\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}u_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}
with
u_{\lambda}\in\left\{-1,0,1\right\}
for
all
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}
. It has this property in common with the
symmetric functions
h_{m}
and
e_{m}
(according to the Pieri rules) and

p_{m}
(according to the Murnaghan–Nakayama rule) as well as several others.
The study of symmetric functions having this property – which we call

MNable symmetric functions
(in honor of Murnaghan and Nakayama) –
has been initiated in
[
Grinbe20a
, §8]
, but there is much to be done.

5.4
A conjecture of Per Alexandersson

In February 2020, Per Alexandersson suggested the following conjecture:

Conjecture 5.1
.

Let
k
be a positive integer, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

G\left(k,m\right)\cdot p_{2}\in\Lambda
can be written in the form

\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}u_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}
with
u_{\lambda}\in\left\{-1,0,1\right\}
for all
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}
.

For example,

G\left(3,4\right)\cdot p_{2}=s_{\left(1,1,1,1,1,1\right)}+s_{\left(2,2,2\right)}-s_{\left(3,1,1,1\right)}-s_{\left(3,3\right)}+s_{\left(4,2\right)}.

Conjecture
5.1
has been verified for all
k
and
m
satisfying

k+m\leq 30
.

Note that Conjecture
5.1
becomes false if
p_{2}
is replaced by

p_{3}
. For example,

G\left(3,4\right)\cdot p_{3}=-s_{\left(1,1,1,1,1,1,1\right)}+s_{\left(2,2,1,1,1\right)}-2s_{\left(2,2,2,1\right)}+s_{\left(3,2,1,1\right)}-s_{\left(4,1,1,1\right)}-s_{\left(4,3\right)}+s_{\left(5,2\right)}.

5.5
A conjecture of François Bergeron

An even more mysterious conjecture was suggested by François Bergeron in
April 2020:

Conjecture 5.2
.

Let
k
and
n
be positive integers, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\nabla
be the
nabla operator
as defined (e.g.) in

[
Berger19
, §3.2.1]
. Then, there exists a sign
\sigma_{n,k,m}\in\left\{1,-1\right\}
such that
\sigma_{n,k,m}\nabla^{n}\left(G\left(k,m\right)\right)
is an
\mathbb{N}\left[q,t\right]
-linear
combination of Schur functions.

Using SageMath, this conjecture has been checked for
n=1
and all

k,m\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,9\right\}
; the signs
\sigma_{1,k,m}
are given
by the following table:

1

(where the entry in the row indexed
k
and the column indexed
