is the sign

\sigma_{1,k,m}
, represented by a “
+
” sign if it is
1
and by a “
-
” sign if
it is
-1
). I am not aware of a pattern in these signs, apart from the fact
that
\sigma_{1,2,m}=1
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
(a consequence of Haiman’s
famous interpretation of
\nabla\left(e_{m}\right)
as a character), and
that
\sigma_{1,k,m}
appears to be
\left(-1\right)^{m-1}
whenever

1\leq m<k
(which would follow from the conjecture that
\left(-1\right)^{m-1}\nabla\left(h_{m}\right)
is an
\mathbb{N}\left[q,t\right]
-linear combination of Schur functions for any
m\geq 1
).

5.6
“Petriefication” of Schur
functions

Theorem
2.27
shows the existence of a Hopf algebra homomorphism

V_{k}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
that sends the complete homogeneous
symmetric functions
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},\ldots
to the Petrie symmetric
functions
G\left(k,1\right),G\left(k,2\right),G\left(k,3\right),\ldots
. It thus is natural to consider the images of all Schur functions

s_{\lambda}
under this homomorphism
V_{k}
. Experiments with small

\lambda
’s may suggest that these images
V_{k}\left(s_{\lambda}\right)

all can be written in the form
\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}u_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}
with
u_{\lambda}\in\left\{-1,0,1\right\}
. But
this is not generally the case; counterexamples include
V_{3}\left(s_{\left(4,4,4\right)}\right)
,
V_{4}\left(s_{\left(4,4\right)}\right)
and
V_{4}\left(s_{\left(5,1,1,1,1\right)}\right)
. (Of
course, it is true when
\lambda
is a single row, because of
V_{k}\left(s_{\left(m\right)}\right)=V_{k}\left(h_{m}\right)=G\left(k,m\right)
; and it is also true when
\lambda
is a single column, because
the Hopf algebra homomorphism
V_{k}
commutes with the antipode
S
that
sends
h_{m}\mapsto\left(-1\right)^{m}e_{m}
and
s_{\lambda}\mapsto\left(-1\right)^{\left|\lambda\right|}s_{\lambda^{t}}
.)

Note that these images
V_{k}\left(s_{\lambda}\right)
are precisely the

modular Schur functions

s_{\lambda}^{\prime}
studied in

[
Walker94
]
.

5.7
Postnikov’s generalization

At the MIT Algebraic Combinatorics preseminar roundtable (2020), Alexander
Postnikov has suggested a generalization of the Petrie symmetric functions
that preserves some of their more elementary properties. In this subsection,
we shall survey this generalization.

Proofs will be sketched (at best); the reader can find the details in the
detailed version
[
Grinbe20b
]
.

Convention 5.1
.

We fix a formal power series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is
1
. (We will keep this
F
fixed throughout the
present subsection.)

The notations in the following definition will also be used throughout this subsection:

Definition 5.3
.

(a)
Let
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
be the
coefficients of the formal power series
F
, so that
F=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}t^{n}
. Thus,
f_{0}
is the constant term of
F
; hence,
f_{0}=1

(since the constant term of
F
is
1
).

(b)
We set
f_{i}=0
for each negative integer
i
.

(c)
For any weak composition
\alpha
, we define an element

f_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{k}
by

f_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha_{1}}f_{\alpha_{2}}f_{\alpha_{3}}\cdots.

(Here, the infinite product
f_{\alpha_{1}}f_{\alpha_{2}}f_{\alpha_{3}}\cdots

is well-defined, since every sufficiently high positive integer
i
satisfies

\alpha_{i}=0
and thus
f_{\alpha_{i}}=f_{0}=1
.)

(d)
We define the power series

G_{F}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}f_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}.

(97)

This is a formal power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
.

(e)
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the power series

G_{F,m}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}.

(98)

This is a formal power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
.

Example 5.4
.

Let us see how these power series
G_{F}
and
G_{F,m}

look for specific values of
F
.

(a)
Let
F=\dfrac{1}{1-t}=1+t+t^{2}+t^{3}+\cdots
. Then,
f_{i}=1

for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
f_{\alpha}=\underbrace{1\cdot 1\cdot 1\cdot\cdots}_{=1}=1
for any weak composition
\alpha
. Thus,

G_{F}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}\underbrace{f_{\alpha}}_{=1}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

and

G_{F,m}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}\underbrace{f_{\alpha}}_{=1}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=h_{m}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }m\in\mathbb{N}.

(b)
Now, let
F=1
. Then,
f_{i}=\left[i=0\right]
for each

i\in\mathbb{N}
(where we are using the Iverson bracket notation from
Convention
2.1
). Hence,
f_{\alpha}=\left[\alpha=\varnothing\right]
for any weak composition
\alpha
. Thus, it is easy to
see that
G_{F}=1
and
G_{F,m}=\left[m=0\right]
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)
Now, fix a positive integer
k
, and set
F=1+t+t^{2}+\cdots+t^{k-1}
. Then,
f_{i}=\left[i<k\right]
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
f_{\alpha}=\prod\limits_{i\geq 1}\left[\alpha_{i}<k\right]=\left[\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]
for any weak composition
\alpha
.
Thus,

\displaystyle G_{F}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}\underbrace{f_{\alpha}}_{=\left[\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}\left[\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all
}i\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since the }\left[\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\right]\text{
factor}\\
\text{makes all addends that don't}\\
\text{satisfy \textquotedblleft}\alpha_{i}<k\text{ for all }i\text{\textquotedblright\ vanish}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=G\left(k\right).

Likewise, we can see that
G_{F,m}=G\left(k,m\right)
for each

m\in\mathbb{N}
. This shows that the
G_{F}
and the
G_{F,m}
are
generalizations of the Petrie symmetric series
G\left(k\right)
and the
Petrie symmetric functions
G\left(k,m\right)
, respectively.

The next proposition generalizes parts
(a)
,
(b)
and

(c)
of Proposition
2.3
:

Proposition 5.5
.

(a)
The formal power series
G_{F,m}
is the

m
-th degree homogeneous component of
G_{F}
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(b)
We have

G_{F}=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC}}f_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}f_{\lambda}m_{\lambda}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right).

(c)
We have

G_{F,m}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}m_{\lambda}\in\Lambda

for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(d)
The formal power series
G_{F}
is symmetric.

(e)
We have
G_{F,0}=1
.

Proof 5.6

(Proof of Proposition
5.5
(sketched).)
.

Parts
(a)
,

(b)
and
(c)
of Proposition
5.5
generalize
the corresponding parts of Proposition
2.3
, and are proved
more or less analogously. (The only novelty is the use of a fact that says
that
f_{\alpha}=f_{\lambda}
whenever a weak composition
\alpha
is obtained
by permuting the entries of a partition
\lambda
. Of course, this fact
follows from the definitions of
f_{\alpha}
and
f_{\lambda}
.)

Part
(d)
of Proposition
5.5
is clear from part

(b)
. Part
(e)
follows from
f_{\varnothing}=1
.

Next, let us generalize Definition
2.4
:

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\right)\in\operatorname*{Par}
and
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{\ell}\right)\in\operatorname*{Par}
. Then, the

F
-Petrie number

\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)
of
\lambda
and
\mu
is the element of
\mathbf{k}
defined
by

\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\det\left(\left(f_{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right).

(99)

Note that this integer does not depend on the choice of
\ell
(in the sense
that it does not change if we enlarge
\ell
by adding trailing zeroes to the
representations of
\lambda
and
\mu
); this follows from Lemma

5.9
below.

Example 5.8
.

For
\ell=3
, the equality (
99
) rewrites as

\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)=\det\left(\begin{array}[c]{ccc}f_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{1}}&f_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{2}+1}&f_{\lambda_{1}-\mu_{3}+2}\\
f_{\lambda_{2}-\mu_{1}-1}&f_{\lambda_{2}-\mu_{2}}&f_{\lambda_{2}-\mu_{3}+1}\\
f_{\lambda_{3}-\mu_{1}-2}&f_{\lambda_{3}-\mu_{2}-1}&f_{\lambda_{3}-\mu_{3}}\end{array}\right).

We can now state the generalization of Lemma
2.6
that is
needed to justify Definition
5.7
:

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}
and
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}\right)
and
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{\ell}\right)
. Then, the determinant

\det\left(\left(f_{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}-i+j}\right)_{1\leq i\leq\ell,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell}\right)
does not depend on the choice of
\ell
.

The slickest way to prove Lemma
5.9
is using a

\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}

that generalizes the homomorphism
\alpha_{k}
from Definition

3.10
. Let us introduce this homomorphism
\alpha_{F}
(which will
also be used in other proofs). We recall the h-universal property of
\Lambda
, which was stated in Subsection
3.5
.

Definition 5.10
.

The h-universal property of
\Lambda
shows that there is a
unique
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
that sends
h_{i}
to
f_{i}
for all positive integers
i
.
Consider this
\alpha_{F}
.

We will use this homomorphism
\alpha_{F}
several times in what follows; let
us thus begin by stating some elementary properties of
\alpha_{F}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

(a)
We have

\alpha_{F}\left(h_{i}\right)=f_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }i\in\mathbb{N}.

(100)

(b)
We have

\alpha_{F}\left(h_{i}\right)=f_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }i\in\mathbb{Z}.

(101)

(c)
Let
\lambda
be a partition. Define
h_{\lambda}
as in
Definition
3.1
. Then,

\alpha_{F}\left(h_{\lambda}\right)=f_{\lambda}.

(102)

Proof 5.12

(Proof of Lemma
5.11
(sketched).)
.

Analogous to the proof of Lemma

3.11
.

Proof 5.13

(Proof of Lemma
5.9
(sketched).)
.

Adapt any of the two
proofs of Lemma
2.6
. (In adapting the first proof, use

\alpha_{F}
instead of
\alpha_{k}
.)

We now come to more substantive properties of
G\left(k\right)
and

G\left(k,m\right)
.

The following theorem generalizes Theorem
2.8
:

Theorem 5.14
.

We have

G_{F}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

(Recall that
\varnothing
denotes the empty partition
\left({}\right)=\left(0,0,0,\ldots\right)
.)

The following corollary (which already appeared in
[
Stanle01
, Exercise 7.91
(d)]
) generalizes Corollary
2.9
:

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

G_{F,m}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{m}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\varnothing\right)s_{\lambda}.

The following theorem generalizes Theorem
2.16
:

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}
. Then,

G_{F}\cdot s_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}.

The following corollary generalizes Corollary
2.17
:

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mu\in\operatorname*{Par}
.
Then,

G_{F,m}\cdot s_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}\nolimits_{m+\left|\mu\right|}}\operatorname*{pet}\nolimits_{F}\left(\lambda,\mu\right)s_{\lambda}.

Proof 5.18

(Proofs of Theorem
5.16
, Corollary
5.17
, Theorem

5.14
and Corollary
5.15
(sketched).)
.

These proofs
are analogous to the proofs of Theorem
2.16
, Corollary

2.17
, Theorem
2.8
and Corollary
2.9
, respectively (but using
\alpha_{F}
instead of
\alpha_{k}
).

Proposition
5.5

(c)
shows that
G_{F,m}\in\Lambda

for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, we can apply the comultiplication
\Delta

of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
to
G_{F,m}
. The next theorem (which
generalizes Theorem
2.18
) gives a simple expression for the
result of this:

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

\Delta\left(G_{F,m}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}G_{F,i}\otimes G_{F,m-i}.

Proof 5.20

(Proof of Theorem
5.19
(sketched).)
.

Proposition

5.5

(b)
tells us that
G_{F}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right)
. Comparing this with the equality
G_{F}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}
(which follows from Proposition
5.5

(a)
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right).

(103)

Substituting the variables
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
for the variables

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
in this equality, we obtain

\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(y_{i}\right).

(104)

Substituting the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots

for the variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
on both sides of the equality

G_{F}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right)
, we obtain

\displaystyle G_{F}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.DeltaGFm.short.1}))}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(y_{i}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.DeltaGFm.short.1y}))}\end{subarray}}=\left(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\underbrace{G_{F,k}}_{=G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\left(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}}G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,j}\left(\mathbf{y}\right).

Comparing the
m
-th degree homogeneous components of both sides of this
equality, we find

G_{F,m}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N};\\
i+j=m\end{subarray}}G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,j}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)

(since Proposition
5.5

(a)
shows that the
m
-th
degree homogeneous component of
G_{F}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)

is
G_{F,m}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)
, while that of the sum

\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}}\underbrace{G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,j}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)}_{\text{homogeneous of degree }i+j}
is the subsum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N};\\
i+j=m\end{subarray}}G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,j}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)
). Thus,

G_{F,m}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N};\\
i+j=m\end{subarray}}G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,j}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}G_{F,i}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)G_{F,m-i}\left(\mathbf{y}\right).

Hence, (
10
) holds for
f=G_{F,m}
,
I=\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}
,
\left(f_{1,i}\right)_{i\in I}=\left(G_{F,i}\right)_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}
and
\left(f_{2,i}\right)_{i\in I}=\left(G_{F,m-i}\right)_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}
. Therefore, (
9
) (applied to
these
f
,
I
,
\left(f_{1,i}\right)_{i\in I}
and
\left(f_{2,i}\right)_{i\in I}
) yields

\Delta\left(G_{F,m}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,m\right\}}G_{F,i}\otimes G_{F,m-i}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}G_{F,i}\otimes G_{F,m-i}.

This proves Theorem
5.19
.

The next few results we will state rely on the following definition:

Definition 5.21
.

Let
F^{\prime}
be the derivative of the formal power
series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
. Let us write the
formal power series
\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(which is well-defined, since
F
has constant term
1
)
in the form
\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}t^{n}
for
some
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots\in\mathbf{k}
.

Example 5.22
.

Let us see how
F^{\prime}
and
\gamma_{n}
look for specific values of
F
.

(a)
Let
F=\dfrac{1}{1-t}=1+t+t^{2}+t^{3}+\cdots
. Then,
F^{\prime}=\dfrac{1}{\left(1-t\right)^{2}}
, so that

\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}=\dfrac{1}{1-t}=1+t+t^{2}+t^{3}+\cdots=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}.

Therefore,
\gamma_{n}=1
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(b)
Now, let
F=1
. Then,
F^{\prime}=0
, so that
\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}=0=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}0t^{n}
. Therefore,
\gamma_{n}=0
for each

n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)
Now, fix a positive integer
k
, and set
F=1+t+t^{2}+\cdots+t^{k-1}
. Then,
F=\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}
, and thus a simple
calculation using the quotient rule shows that
F^{\prime}=\dfrac{1+\left(k-1\right)t^{k}-kt^{k-1}}{\left(1-t\right)^{2}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1+\left(k-1\right)t^{k}-kt^{k-1}}{\left(1-t\right)\left(1-t^{k}\right)}=\underbrace{\dfrac{1}{1-t}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}}-kt^{k-1}\cdot\underbrace{\dfrac{1}{1-t^{k}}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(t^{k}\right)^{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}-\underbrace{kt^{k-1}\cdot\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(t^{k}\right)^{n}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}kt^{nk+k-1}\\
=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\mid n+1\end{subarray}}kt^{n}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N};\\
k\mid n+1\end{subarray}}kt^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(1-\left[k\mid n+1\right]k\right)t^{n}.

Therefore,
\gamma_{n}=1-\left[k\mid n+1\right]k
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

The next proposition is easily seen to generalize Proposition

2.23
:

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
m
be a positive integer. Then,
\left\langle p_{m},G_{F,m}\right\rangle=\gamma_{m-1}
.

The proof of this proposition relies on the following property of the

\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}

from Definition
5.10
:

Lemma 5.24
.

We have
\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m}\right)=\gamma_{m-1}

for each positive integer
m
.

Proof 5.25

(Proof of Lemma
5.24
(sketched).)
.

Consider the ring

\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of formal power series in one
indeterminate
t
over
\Lambda
. Consider also the analogous ring

\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
over
\mathbf{k}
.

The map
\alpha_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbf{k}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism, and therefore induces a
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism

\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]:\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]

that sends each formal power series
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(with
a_{n}\in\Lambda
) to
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\alpha_{F}\left(a_{n}\right)t^{n}
. Consider this
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

Define the formal power series

H\left(t\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(1-x_{i}t\right)^{-1}\in\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right].

(105)

Then, from
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.1)]
, we know that

H\left(t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\underbrace{h_{n}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=h_{n}}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right].

It is now easy to see that

\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)=F.

(106)

(Indeed, this follows by straightforward computations using the definition of

\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
from
H\left(t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}
and from Lemma
5.11

(a)
.)

Also, it is easy to see that the map
\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
respects derivatives: i.e., any power series
u\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
satisfies
\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(u^{\prime}\right)=\left(\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(u\right)\right)^{\prime}
. Applying this to
u=H\left(t\right)
, we obtain

\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H^{\prime}\left(t\right)\right)=\left(\underbrace{\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)}_{=F}\right)^{\prime}=F^{\prime}.

(107)

From
[
GriRei20
, Exercise 2.5.21]
, we know that

\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}p_{m+1}t^{m}=\dfrac{H^{\prime}\left(t\right)}{H\left(t\right)}.

(108)

Applying the map
\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
to both sides
of this equality, we find

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}p_{m+1}t^{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\dfrac{H^{\prime}\left(t\right)}{H\left(t\right)}\right)=\dfrac{\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H^{\prime}\left(t\right)\right)}{\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\text{ is a }\mathbf{k}\text{-algebra
homomorphism}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.alphaF.p.short.5}) and (\ref{pf.lem.alphaF.p.short.5pri})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\gamma_{n}t^{n}.

Comparing this with

\left(\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}p_{m+1}t^{m}\right)=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m+1}\right)t^{m}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}\alpha_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right),

we obtain

\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m+1}\right)t^{m}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\gamma_{n}t^{n}.

Comparing
t^{n}
-coefficients on both sides of this equality, we find

\alpha_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)=\gamma_{n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

In other words,
\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m}\right)=\gamma_{m-1}
for each
positive integer
m
. This proves Lemma
5.24
.

Proof 5.26

(Proof of Proposition
5.23
(sketched).)
.

Proposition

5.5

(c)
yields
G_{F,m}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}m_{\lambda}
. Hence,

\left\langle p_{m},G_{F,m}\right\rangle=\left\langle p_{m},\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}m_{\lambda}\right\rangle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle

(109)

(since the Hall inner product is
\mathbf{k}
-bilinear).

Now, recall that the bases
\left(m_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}
and
\left(h_{\lambda}\right)_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}
of
\Lambda
are dual to each other with respect to
the Hall inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
. Hence, every

a\in\Lambda
satisfies

a=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\left\langle m_{\lambda},a\right\rangle h_{\lambda}

(by a general property of dual bases with respect to symmetric bilinear
forms). Applying this to
a=p_{m}
, we obtain

\displaystyle p_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par}}\left\langle m_{\lambda},p_{m}\right\rangle h_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left\langle m_{\lambda},p_{m}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle\\
\text{(since the Hall inner}\\
\text{product is symmetric)}\end{subarray}}h_{\lambda}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|\neq m\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left\langle m_{\lambda},p_{m}\right\rangle}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.Hall.graded}), since }m_{\lambda}\text{ and
}p_{m}\\
\text{are homogeneous}\\
\text{of degrees }\left|\lambda\right|\text{ and }m\\
\text{(and since }\left|\lambda\right|\neq m\text{))}\end{subarray}}h_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle h_{\lambda}.

Applying the map
\alpha_{F}
to both sides of this equality, we find

\displaystyle\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle\underbrace{\alpha_{F}\left(h_{\lambda}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=f_{\lambda}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.alphaF.h}
{(c)})}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the map }\alpha_{F}\text{ is }\mathbf{k}\text{-linear}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle f_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=m\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}\left\langle p_{m},m_{\lambda}\right\rangle.

Comparing this with (
109
), we obtain

\left\langle p_{m},G_{F,m}\right\rangle=\alpha_{F}\left(p_{m}\right)=\gamma_{m-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.alphaF.p}}\right).

This proves Proposition
5.23
.

We can now generalize Theorem
2.21
:

Theorem 5.27
.

Assume that all the elements
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots
are invertible in
\mathbf{k}
.

Then, the family
\left(G_{F,m}\right)_{m\geq 1}=\left(G_{F,1},G_{F,2},G_{F,3},\ldots\right)
is an algebraically independent generating
set of the commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\Lambda
. (In other words, the
canonical
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\left[u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},\ldots\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda,

\displaystyle u_{m}

\displaystyle\mapsto G_{F,m}

is an isomorphism.)

Proof 5.28

(Proof of Theorem
5.27
(sketched).)
.

Analogous to the proof of
Theorem
2.21
, but using Proposition
5.23
(and
Proposition
5.5
) instead of Proposition
2.23

(and Proposition
2.3
).

Remark 5.29
.

It is not hard to verify that the converse of Theorem
5.27

also holds: If the family
\left(G_{F,m}\right)_{m\geq 1}=\left(G_{F,1},G_{F,2},G_{F,3},\ldots\right)
generates the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra

\Lambda
, then all the elements
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots
are
invertible in
\mathbf{k}
. We omit the proof of this.

The next theorem generalizes parts of Theorem
2.27
(specifically,
it generalizes the properties of the map
V_{k}
stated in Theorem

2.27
, even though it defines this map differently):
38
38

38

We
recall the “h-universal property of
\Lambda
”, which we stated in Subsection

3.5
.

Theorem 5.30
.

The h-universal property of
\Lambda
shows that there is a
unique
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
V_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda

that sends
h_{i}
to
G_{F,i}
for all positive integers
i
(since

G_{F,i}\in\Lambda
for each positive integer
i
). Consider this
V_{F}
.

(a)
This map
V_{F}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-Hopf algebra homomorphism.

(b)
We have
V_{F}\left(h_{m}\right)=G_{F,m}
for each

m\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)
We have
V_{F}\left(p_{n}\right)=\gamma_{n-1}p_{n}
for each
positive integer
n
. (See Definition
5.21
for the meaning of

\gamma_{n-1}
.)

Proof 5.31

(Proof of Theorem
5.30
(sketched).)
.

(b)
When
m
is
positive, this follows from the very definition of
V_{F}
. It remains to
prove this for
m=0
. However, this boils down to showing that
V_{F}\left(1\right)=1
, which is clear (since
V_{F}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism).

(a)
Let
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
be the comultiplication and the
counit of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
. Both
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are

\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms. It suffices to show that
\Delta\circ V_{F}=\left(V_{F}\otimes V_{F}\right)\circ\Delta
and
\varepsilon\circ V_{F}=\varepsilon
. We shall show that
\Delta\circ V_{F}=\left(V_{F}\otimes V_{F}\right)\circ\Delta
only; the proof of
\varepsilon\circ V_{F}=\varepsilon
is similar but much simpler (since
\varepsilon
sends any
homogeneous symmetric function of positive degree to
0
).

Recall that the family
\left(h_{n}\right)_{n\geq 1}
generates
\Lambda

as a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra. Thus, in order to prove that
\Delta\circ V_{F}=\left(V_{F}\otimes V_{F}\right)\circ\Delta
, it suffices to prove
the equality
\left(\Delta\circ V_{F}\right)\left(h_{n}\right)=\left(\left(V_{F}\otimes V_{F}\right)\circ\Delta\right)\left(h_{n}\right)

for each
n\geq 1
(since both
\Delta\circ V_{F}
and
\left(V_{F}\otimes V_{F}\right)\circ\Delta
are
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphisms). In view of
Theorem
5.30

(b)
, this equality rewrites as

\Delta\left(G_{F,n}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{F,i}\otimes G_{F,n-i}
. But
this follows directly from Theorem
5.19
.

(c)
This is best proved using the notion of a logarithmic derivative.
Let us first define it in full generality, without any assumptions on

\mathbf{k}
.

If
R
is a commutative ring, and if
F\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
is any formal power series whose constant term is
1
(or, more generally,
any formal power series that has a multiplicative inverse), then the

logarithmic derivative
of
F
is defined to be the formal power series

\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(this is
well-defined, since
F
is invertible). This logarithmic derivative is denoted
by
\operatorname*{lder}F
.

The following properties of logarithmic derivatives are easy to
prove
39
39

39

If
R
is a commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, then the
logarithmic derivative
\operatorname*{lder}F
of a power series
F\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
equals the derivative of
\log F
. This
trivializes many of the properties stated below; but this shortcut is not
available when
R
is merely an arbitrary commutative ring.
:

1.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Let
u,v\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be two formal power series whose constant terms are
1
. Then,

\operatorname*{lder}\left(uv\right)=\operatorname*{lder}u+\operatorname*{lder}v
.

(
Proof:
Just recall the definition of logarithmic derivatives and the
Leibniz law
\left(uv\right)^{\prime}=u^{\prime}v+uv^{\prime}
.)

2.

Let
R
be a commutative topological ring. Let
\left(u_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},\ldots\right)\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]^{\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of formal power series
whose constant terms are
1
. Let
u\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]

be a formal power series whose constant term is
1
. Assume that

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}=u
(with respect to the standard
topology on
R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
induced by the topology on

R
). Then,
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\operatorname*{lder}u_{n}\right)=\operatorname*{lder}u
(with respect to the same topology on

R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
).

(
Proof:
Let
R\left[\left[t\right]\right]_{1}
be the set of
power series in
R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is

1
. Argue that
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(u_{n}^{\prime}\right)=u^{\prime}
first; then argue that the map

\displaystyle R\left[\left[t\right]\right]\times R\left[\left[t\right]\right]_{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow R\left[\left[t\right]\right],

\displaystyle\left(v,w\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\dfrac{v}{w}

is continuous.)

3.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Let
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be finitely many formal power series whose
constant terms are
1
. Then,

\operatorname*{lder}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname*{lder}u_{i}.

(
Proof:
Induction on
n
, using Property 1 in the induction step.)

4.

Let
R
be a commutative topological ring. Let
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},\ldots\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be infinitely many formal
power series whose constant terms are
1
. Assume that the infinite product

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}u_{i}
converges (with respect to the standard topology
on
R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
induced by the topology on
R
).
Then, the infinite sum
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\operatorname*{lder}u_{i}

converges as well, and we have

\operatorname*{lder}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}u_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\operatorname*{lder}u_{i}.

(
Proof:
This is the “
n\rightarrow\infty
” limit of Property 3. Use Property 2 to pass to this limit.)

5.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Let
u\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be a formal power series whose constant term is
1
. Let

\lambda\in R
. Then,

\operatorname*{lder}\left(u\left(\lambda t\right)\right)=\lambda\cdot\left(\operatorname*{lder}u\right)\left(\lambda t\right).

(
Proof:
This follows from the equality
\left(u\left(\lambda t\right)\right)^{\prime}=\lambda\cdot u^{\prime}\left(\lambda t\right)
, which is an easy consequence of the chain rule but also easy to check directly.)

6.

Let
R
and
S
be two commutative
\mathbf{k}
-algebras. Let

\alpha:R\rightarrow S
be a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism. As we know,

\alpha
induces a
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra
homomorphism

\alpha\left[\left[t\right]\right]:R\left[\left[t\right]\right]\rightarrow S\left[\left[t\right]\right]

that sends each power series
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(with
a_{n}\in R
) to
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\alpha\left(a_{n}\right)t^{n}\in S\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

Let
u\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be a formal power series whose
constant term is
1
. Then, the constant term of the power series
\left(\alpha\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(u\right)
is
1
,
and we have

\operatorname*{lder}\left(\left(\alpha\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(u\right)\right)=\left(\alpha\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\operatorname*{lder}u\right).

(
Proof:
This is essentially saying that the logarithmic derivative is
functorial with respect to the base ring. The proof is straightforward.)

Now, let us come back to proving Theorem
5.30

(c)
:

Consider the ring
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
of
formal power series in one indeterminate
t
over
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
. This ring is a topological ring,
where the topology is the standard one induced by the standard topology on

\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
(not
the discrete topology!). This topological ring
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
is, of course, isomorphic to
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,t\right]\right]
. The ring
\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
is a subring of
\left(\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]\right)\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

Now, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we know that
G_{F,m}
is homogeneous of
degree
m
(by Proposition
5.5

(a)
), and therefore
satisfies

G_{F,m}\left(tx_{1},tx_{2},tx_{3},\ldots\right)=t^{m}\cdot G_{F,m}

(110)

(since any formal power series
u\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
that is homogeneous of degree
m

satisfies
u\left(tx_{1},tx_{2},tx_{3},\ldots\right)=t^{m}\cdot u
).

On the other hand, from (
103
), we obtain

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,k}=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,m}.

Substituting
tx_{1},tx_{2},tx_{3},\ldots
for
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
on
both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(tx_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\underbrace{G_{F,m}\left(tx_{1},tx_{2},tx_{3},\ldots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t^{m}\cdot G_{F,m}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.VF.main.short.c.GFmtxi}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}t^{m}\cdot G_{F,m}.

(111)

The map
V_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
is a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism. Hence, it induces a
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
-algebra homomorphism

V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]:\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]\rightarrow\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]

that sends each formal power series
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right]
(with
a_{n}\in\Lambda
) to
\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}V_{F}\left(a_{n}\right)t^{n}
. Consider this
V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
.

Define the formal power series
H\left(t\right)
as in
(
105
). Then, from
[
GriRei20
, (2.4.1)]
, we know
that

H\left(t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}\underbrace{h_{n}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}_{=h_{n}}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\in\Lambda\left[\left[t\right]\right].

Moreover,
H\left(t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}
shows that the
constant term of
H\left(t\right)
is
h_{0}=1
. Thus,

\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)
is well-defined.

Applying the map
V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
to both sides of
the equality
H\left(t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}h_{n}t^{n}\right)=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\underbrace{V_{F}\left(h_{n}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=G_{F,n}\\
\text{(by Theorem \ref{thm.VF.main} {(b)})}\end{subarray}}t^{n}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G_{F,n}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t^{n}\cdot G_{F,n}=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}t^{m}\cdot G_{F,m}.

Comparing this with (
111
), we find

\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(tx_{i}\right).

(112)

Now, the definition of
\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)
yields

\displaystyle\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{H^{\prime}\left(t\right)}{H\left(t\right)}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}p_{m+1}t^{m}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.lem.alphaF.p.short.pHH})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}p_{n+1}t^{n}.

Applying the map
V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
to both sides of
this equality, we find

\displaystyle\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}p_{n+1}t^{n}\right)=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)t^{n}.

(113)

Now is the time to use our above list of properties of logarithmic
derivatives. Recall that the constant term of
H\left(t\right)
is
1
.
Hence, Property 6 of logarithmic derivatives shows that the constant term of
the power series
\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)
is
1
, and that we have

\operatorname*{lder}\left(\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)\right)=\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)\right).

(114)

Now, (
113
) yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(\operatorname*{lder}\left(H\left(t\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{lder}\left(\left(V_{F}\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)\left(H\left(t\right)\right)\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.thm.VF.main.short.c.4})}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{lder}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(tx_{i}\right)\right)

(115)

(by (
112
)).

Now, the infinite product
\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(tx_{i}\right)

converges (as we know from (
111
)). Hence,
Property 4 of logarithmic derivatives yields that the infinite sum
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\operatorname*{lder}\left(F\left(tx_{i}\right)\right)

converges as well, and that we have

\operatorname*{lder}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(tx_{i}\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\operatorname*{lder}\left(F\left(tx_{i}\right)\right).

Hence, (
115
) rewrites as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\operatorname*{lder}\left(F\left(\underbrace{tx_{i}}_{=x_{i}t}\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\operatorname*{lder}\left(F\left(x_{i}t\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{i}\cdot\left(\operatorname*{lder}F\right)\left(x_{i}t\right)\\
\text{(by Property 5}\\
\text{of logarithmic derivatives)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}\cdot\left(\operatorname*{lder}F\right)\left(x_{i}t\right).

(116)

The definition of
\operatorname*{lder}F
yields

\operatorname*{lder}F=\dfrac{F^{\prime}}{F}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}t^{n}.

Hence, for each
i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
, we have

\left(\operatorname*{lder}F\right)\left(x_{i}t\right)=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}\underbrace{\left(x_{i}t\right)^{n}}_{=x_{i}^{n}t^{n}}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n}t^{n}.

(117)

Now, (
116
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\operatorname*{lder}F\right)\left(x_{i}t\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n}t^{n}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.VF.main.short.c.8}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}\cdot\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n}t^{n}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\ \ \sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}_{=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}}\underbrace{x_{i}\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n}}_{=\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n+1}}t^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \ \sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}x_{i}^{n+1}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}\underbrace{\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}^{n+1}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{1}^{n+1}+x_{2}^{n+1}+x_{3}^{n+1}+\cdots\\
=p_{n+1}\\
\text{(by the definition of }p_{n+1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{n}p_{n+1}t^{n}.

Comparing coefficients before
t^{n}
in this equality, we conclude that

V_{F}\left(p_{n+1}\right)=\gamma_{n}p_{n+1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for
each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

In other words,
V_{F}\left(p_{n}\right)=\gamma_{n-1}p_{n}
for each
positive integer
n
. This proves Theorem
5.30

(c)
.

Our next (and last) few results are not generalizations of any properties of
Petrie functions. To state them, we take a somewhat more high-level point of
view. We forget that we fixed the power series
F
. Instead, for

every
power series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is
1
, we define a power series
G_{F}
according to
Definition
5.3

(d)
. Moreover, for
every
power
series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term
is
1
, and for
every

m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a power series

G_{F,m}
according to Definition
5.3

(e)
. We then have
the following:

Proposition 5.32
.

Let
A
and
B
be two power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant terms are
1
. Then:

(a)
We have
G_{AB}=G_{A}G_{B}
.

(b)
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We have
G_{AB,n}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{A,i}G_{B,n-i}
.

Proof 5.33

(Proof of Proposition
5.32
(sketched).)
.

The power series
AB
has
constant term
1
(since
A
and
B
have constant term
1
). Thus,
G_{AB}

is well-defined, as is
G_{AB,n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(a)
Proposition
5.5

(b)
yields that

G_{F}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}F\left(x_{i}\right)
for any power series

F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is

1
. Applying this to
F=A
and to
F=B
and to
F=AB
yields
G_{A}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}A\left(x_{i}\right)
and
G_{B}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}B\left(x_{i}\right)
and

G_{AB}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{\left(AB\right)\left(x_{i}\right)}_{=A\left(x_{i}\right)B\left(x_{i}\right)}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(A\left(x_{i}\right)B\left(x_{i}\right)\right)=\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}A\left(x_{i}\right)\right)}_{=G_{A}}\underbrace{\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}B\left(x_{i}\right)\right)}_{=G_{B}}=G_{A}G_{B}.

This proves Proposition
5.32

(a)
.

(b)
Proposition
5.32

(a)
yields
G_{AB}=G_{A}G_{B}
. Thus, the
n
-th degree homogeneous components of
G_{AB}
and
of
G_{A}G_{B}
are equal. But this means precisely that
G_{AB,n}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{A,i}G_{B,n-i}
(by Proposition
5.5

(a)
). This proves Proposition
5.32

(b)
.

Finally, we can express the image of the symmetric function
G_{F,n}
under
the antipode of
\Lambda
(a result suggested by Sasha Postnikov):

Theorem 5.34
.

Let
S
be the antipode of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
.
Let
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
be a formal power
series whose constant term is
1
. Then, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

S\left(G_{F,n}\right)=G_{F^{-1},n}.

(118)

Proof 5.35

(Proof of Theorem
5.34
(sketched).)
.

Let
\Delta
and

\varepsilon
be the comultiplication and the counit of the Hopf algebra

\Lambda
. Let
\eta:\mathbf{k}\rightarrow\Lambda
be the map that sends each

u\in\mathbf{k}
to
u\cdot 1_{\Lambda}\in\Lambda
. It is easy to see that each
positive integer
n
satisfies

\varepsilon\left(G_{F,n}\right)=0.

(119)

(Indeed,
\varepsilon
sends each homogeneous symmetric function of positive
degree to
0
; but
G_{F,n}
is a homogeneous symmetric function of degree

n
.) Also, Proposition
5.5

(e)
yields
G_{F,0}=1

and thus
\varepsilon\left(G_{F,0}\right)=1
.

We shall use the convolution
\star
introduced in Definition

2.26
. The antipode
S
of
\Lambda
is the
\star
-inverse of
the map
\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
(by
the definition of the antipode of a Hopf algebra). In other words,

S\star\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}=\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\star S=\eta\circ\varepsilon

(since
\eta\circ\varepsilon:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
is the neutral element
with respect to
\star
). We also have
S\left(1\right)=1
(by one of the
fundamental properties of the antipode of a Hopf algebra).

Now, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\Delta\left(G_{F,n}\right)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{F,i}\otimes G_{F,n-i}

(by Theorem
5.19
) and therefore

\displaystyle\left(S\star\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\right)\left(G_{F,n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}S\left(G_{F,i}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\operatorname*{id}\left(G_{F,n-i}\right)}_{=G_{F,n-i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of convolution}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}S\left(G_{F,i}\right)\cdot G_{F,n-i},

so that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}S\left(G_{F,i}\right)\cdot G_{F,n-i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(S\star\operatorname*{id}\nolimits_{\Lambda}\right)}_{=\eta\circ\varepsilon}\left(G_{F,n}\right)=\left(\eta\circ\varepsilon\right)\left(G_{F,n}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[n=0\right]

(120)

(the last equality sign here follows easily from
(
119
) and from
\varepsilon\left(G_{F,0}\right)=1
).

On the other hand, the constant term of the power series
F^{-1}
is
1

(since the constant term of
F
is
1
). Hence,
G_{F^{-1},n}
is well-defined
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

G_{F^{-1}F,n}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{F^{-1},i}G_{F,n-i}

(by Proposition
5.32

(b)
, applied to
A=F^{-1}
and

B=F
) and thus

\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}G_{F^{-1},i}G_{F,n-i}=G_{F^{-1}F,n}=G_{1,n}=\left[n=0\right]

(121)

(the last equality sign here has been shown in Example
5.4

(b)
).

Recall that
G_{F,0}=1
. Hence, the equalities
(
121
) (for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
) can be
recursively solved for
G_{F^{-1},0},G_{F^{-1},1},G_{F^{-1},2},\ldots

(starting with
G_{F,0},G_{F,1},G_{F,2},\ldots
); we obtain

G_{F^{-1},n}=\left[n=0\right]-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}G_{F^{-1},i}G_{F,n-i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

The same argument, but using the equalities
(
120
) instead of
(
121
), yields

S\left(G_{F,n}\right)=\left[n=0\right]-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}S\left(G_{F,i}\right)\cdot G_{F,n-i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Comparing these two recursive formulas for
G_{F^{-1},n}
and
S\left(G_{F,n}\right)
, we see that they are the same. Thus, by strong induction on

n
, we conclude that

S\left(G_{F,n}\right)=G_{F^{-1},n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{.}

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.34
.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.34
, we obtain a formula for the
antipode of a Petrie symmetric function:

Corollary 5.36
.

Let
k
be a positive integer such that
k>1
. A weak
composition
\alpha
will be called
k
-friendly
if each
i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
satisfies
\alpha_{i}\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k
or

\alpha_{i}\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k
. If
\alpha
is a weak composition,
then
w\left(\alpha\right)
shall denote the number of all
i\in\left\{1,2,3,\ldots\right\}
satisfying
\alpha_{i}\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k
.

Let
S
be the antipode of the Hopf algebra
\Lambda
. Then, for each

n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

S\left(G\left(k,n\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n;\\
\alpha\text{ is
}k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n;\\
\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}m_{\lambda}.

Proof 5.37

(Proof of Corollary
5.36
(sketched).)
.

Let
F=1+t+t^{2}+\cdots+t^{k-1}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
. Then,
F
is
a power series whose constant term is
1
. Hence, its reciprocal
F^{-1}
is
well-defined and again is a power series whose constant term is
1
. Let us
denote this reciprocal
F^{-1}
by
Q
; thus,
Q=F^{-1}
.

Let
q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},\ldots
be the coefficients of the formal power series

Q
, so that
Q=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}q_{n}t^{n}
. Thus,
q_{0}
is the
constant term of
Q
; hence,
q_{0}=1
(since the constant term of
Q
is
1
).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=F^{-1}=\left(\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}\right)^{-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }F=1+t+t^{2}+\cdots+t^{k-1}=\dfrac{1-t^{k}}{1-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1-t}{1-t^{k}}=\left(1-t\right)\cdot\underbrace{\left(1-t^{k}\right)^{-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left(t^{k}\right)^{m}=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{N}}t^{mk}\\
=t^{0}+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots\end{subarray}}=\left(1-t\right)\cdot\left(t^{0}+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(t^{0}+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N};\\
n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\end{subarray}}t^{n}\\
=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left[n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\right]t^{n}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{t\cdot\left(t^{0}+t^{k}+t^{2k}+t^{3k}+\cdots\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=t^{1}+t^{k+1}+t^{2k+1}+t^{3k+1}+\cdots\\
=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N};\\
n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\end{subarray}}t^{n}\\
=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left[n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\right]t^{n}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left[n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\right]t^{n}-\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left[n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\right]t^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\left[n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\right]-\left[n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\right]\right)t^{n}.

Comparing this with
Q=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}q_{n}t^{n}
, we obtain

\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}q_{n}t^{n}=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\left[n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\right]-\left[n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\right]\right)t^{n}.

Comparing coefficients on both sides of this equality, we find

q_{n}=\left[n\equiv 0\operatorname{mod}k\right]-\left[n\equiv 1\operatorname{mod}k\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(122)

For any weak composition
\alpha
, we define an element
q_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{k}
by

q_{\alpha}=q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\alpha_{3}}\cdots.

(Here, the infinite product
q_{\alpha_{1}}q_{\alpha_{2}}q_{\alpha_{3}}\cdots

is well-defined, since every sufficiently high positive integer
i
satisfies

\alpha_{i}=0
and thus
q_{\alpha_{i}}=q_{0}=1
.)

It is now easy to see (using (
122
)) that

q_{\alpha}=\left[\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}

(123)

for any weak composition
\alpha
.

Now, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that our scalars
q_{i}
and
q_{\alpha}

were defined in the exact same way as the scalars
f_{i}
and
f_{\alpha}

were defined in Definition
5.3
, but using the power series
Q

instead of
F
. Hence, Proposition
5.5

(c)
(applied
to
Q
,
q_{i}
,
q_{\alpha}
and
n
instead of
F
,
f_{i}
,
f_{\alpha}

and
m
) yields that

G_{Q,n}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n\end{subarray}}q_{\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n\end{subarray}}q_{\lambda}m_{\lambda}\in\Lambda.

Hence,

\displaystyle G_{Q,n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{q_{\alpha}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.cor.Gkm.antipode.qal=}))}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n\end{subarray}}\left[\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n;\\
\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}

(124)

(since the factor
\left[\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]
inside
the sum makes all the addends vanish except for those that satisfy
“
\alpha
is
k
-friendly”) and

\displaystyle G_{Q,n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{q_{\lambda}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.cor.Gkm.antipode.qal=}),}\\
\text{applied
to }\lambda\\
\text{instead of }\alpha\text{)}\end{subarray}}m_{\lambda}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n\end{subarray}}\left[\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]\cdot\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}m_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n;\\
\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}m_{\lambda}

(125)

(since the factor
\left[\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\right]

inside the sum makes all the addends vanish except for those that satisfy
“
\lambda
is
k
-friendly”).

However, in Example
5.4

(c)
, we have seen that

G_{F,m}=G\left(k,m\right)
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Applying this to

m=n
, we obtain
G_{F,n}=G\left(k,n\right)
. Thus,
G\left(k,n\right)=G_{F,n}
, so that

\displaystyle S\left(G\left(k,n\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S\left(G_{F,n}\right)=G_{F^{-1},n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Theorem
\ref{thm.GF.antipode}}\right)

\displaystyle=G_{Q,n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }F^{-1}=Q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n;\\
\alpha\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.cor.Gkm.antipode.GHn=1})}\right).

Combining this with

S\left(G\left(k,n\right)\right)=G_{Q,n}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n;\\
\lambda\text{ is
}k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}m_{\lambda}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.cor.Gkm.antipode.GHn=2})}\right),

we obtain

S\left(G\left(k,n\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\operatorname*{WC};\\
\left|\alpha\right|=n;\\
\alpha\text{ is
}k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\alpha\right)}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\operatorname*{Par};\\
\left|\lambda\right|=n;\\
\lambda\text{ is }k\text{-friendly}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{w\left(\lambda\right)}m_{\lambda}.

This proves Corollary
5.36
.

One last property of
G_{F,n}
shall be noted in passing:

Proposition 5.38
.

For any power series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is
1
, we define a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
V_{F}:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
as in Theorem

5.30
. Then:

(a)
If
A
and
B
are two power series in
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant terms are
1
, then
V_{AB}=V_{A}\star V_{B}
.

(b)
We have
V_{1}=\eta\circ\varepsilon
.

(c)
For any power series
F\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[t\right]\right]
whose constant term is
1
, we have
V_{F^{-1}}=V_{F}\circ S
,
where
S
is the antipode of
\Lambda
.

We leave the proof of Proposition
5.38
(which follows easily
from Proposition
5.32
) to the reader.
